#10294. ‘Hey Gemini’ hotword debuts on Android XR glasses

Google is finally introducing a “Hey Gemini” hotword for its AI assistant, but it’s only on Android XR glasses to start.

Around a decade after “Hey Google” made its debut, Google is introducing its first new always-listening hotword. “Hey Gemini” makes its debut on Google’s Intelligent Eyewear glasses powered by Android XR, with wearers able to use that hotword in place of “Hey Google.”

This is a move that doesn’t come as a huge surprise. If anything, it’s perhaps a bit overdue.

The move from Assistant to Gemini would have been the expected place for Google to make this change, but it’s one that many will certainly welcome. While the hotword you use really won’t matter much at the end of the day, “Hey Google” has been recognized by many to not roll of the tongue very nicely. It’s a phrase that’s easy to stumble over, where “Hey Gemini” is a bit smoother.

As for whether or not “Hey Gemini” will become the default on future product remains unclear, but it’s a change only available on glasses for now.

We’ve asked Google if both hotwords will be active on Android XR glasses, but it was implied that this is, in fact, the case. When paired to Samsung glasses, “Hey Bixby” may also be in play, as Samsung’s voice assistant is supported on Intelligent Eyewear. Exact functionality, though, is something we’re still waiting for more clarity on.

#10292. Data-Driven Pages That Never Go Stale: A Practical Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every developer has shipped the other kind of page: a lovingly written article or landing page that peaks on launch day and then quietly rots. Screenshots age, statistics expire, rankings slide. Data-driven pages play the opposite game. Built from a living dataset, they republish themselves daily, grow to thousands of URLs, and answer questions nobody had asked when the site launched. This guide breaks down how they work, using a real fan-run stats site as the running example: one public dataset in, more than ten thousand fresh pages out.

Static pages lose ground because the web moves and they do not. Facts expire, competitors publish fresher answers, and search engines reward pages whose information matches today. A page that cannot update itself must be rewritten by hand, so most sites update a few pages and abandon the rest.

Freshness is not one rule for the whole web; it depends on the query. Nobody needs a CSS tutorial rewritten weekly, but statistics, prices, availability and anything with a season attached decays on a schedule. Developers misjudge this constantly, treating every page like documentation when half their catalogue is really a perishable good. Serve a three-month-old stat as if it were current and readers learn the most damaging lesson a site can teach: do not trust the numbers here.

Then there is the maintenance maths. A hundred hand-written pages each needing one review a year is a hundred small debts, and the interest compounds as the catalogue grows. The team updates the flagship pages, promises to return for the rest, and quietly never does. Data-driven architecture is really a staffing decision disguised as a technical one: humans design the system once, and the system does the updating forever.

A data-driven site has three layers: a dataset that refreshes on a schedule, templates that turn records into pages, and URL patterns that map every meaningful combination users might search for. Change the data and every page changes; improve a template and ten thousand pages improve at once.

The running example makes this concrete. A fan-built Overwatch stats tracker republishes Blizzard's public hero data on a daily cycle. From that one feed it generates hero profiles, head-to-head matchup pages and multi-condition tier lists: 10,291 URLs in its sitemap, across six languages, with matchup pages alone accounting for 8,586 of them. Nobody wrote those pages. Somebody designed the system that writes them, which is a very different, and much more scalable, job.

The example runs on Next.js behind a CDN, but the stack is the least important layer of the three. The same pipeline feeding WordPress, Astro or a static site generator would look identical from the reader's side of the glass. Choose boring technology you already know, and spend your originality budget on the dataset and the templates, because that is where readers will feel the difference.

Generated pages stay useful when every URL must earn its existence with data. Set minimum thresholds before a page is published, give each template unique modules its siblings do not have, and noindex combinations with nothing to say. The line between a resource and spam is not automation; it is emptiness.

Quality gates work best when they are public and mechanical. The example site publishes its tier formula openly: 70% of the score from win rate, 30% from pick rate, and a hard floor that excludes heroes picked in under 0.5% of matches from topping the ranking, because a tiny sample cannot prove anything. That single floor is editorial policy expressed as code. Your version might be a minimum number of data points, a minimum price history, or a minimum review count — the number matters less than the fact that the gate exists and applies to every page equally.

Each template also needs modules its siblings cannot copy. A hero profile carries a seven-day trend and its best and worst matchups; a matchup page carries the head-to-head history; a tier list carries the movement since yesterday. Add a short human layer on top — a methodology page explaining the scoring, changelogs narrating what moved — and the catalogue stops reading as output and starts reading as a publication. Google's scaled-content rules target pages manufactured to manipulate rankings with little value; a page that answers a real query with real, current data is the opposite of that. The honest test for any generated URL is simple: if it vanished tonight, would a searcher lose an answer?

Start embarrassingly small: one dataset, one entity type, one template. A football club's results archive, a property portal's sold prices, your own product catalogue — anything structured and refreshed beats a grand plan. Prove one template earns traffic, then add combinations, languages and dashboards in that order.

What is programmatic SEO? Programmatic SEO means generating many pages from a structured dataset using templates, instead of writing each page by hand. Done well, each page answers a distinct search with real data. Done badly, it produces thousands of near-identical pages, which is where its reputation suffers.

Is programmatic SEO against Google's rules? No. Google's spam policies target scaled content created mainly to manipulate rankings, with little or no value for users. Pages generated from real data that answer real queries are publishing. The deciding question is whether a page helps its reader, not how it was produced.

How often should the data behind pages refresh? Match the refresh to how fast the underlying facts change. Sports and game statistics justify daily pulls, prices often need hourly checks, and reference data may only move monthly. Refreshing faster than reality changes wastes crawl budget and makes 'updated' stamps meaningless.

Do generated pages need unique written text? They need unique value, which is not the same thing. Identical paragraphs on ten thousand pages add nothing, but unique data, comparisons and trends are content in their own right. Add short human-written context per template, and let the data carry the differences between pages.

How many pages should a first project launch with? Launch with the smallest set that proves the template: one entity type, a few hundred pages at most. Validate that search engines index them and users engage before adding combinations and languages. Scaling an unproven template just manufactures tomorrow's cleanup job at industrial size.

The sites quietly winning the long game are not publishing more; they are publishing systems. One honest dataset, one well-built template and strict quality gates will outlast a hundred hand-written pages competing on freshness alone. Try it this week: take one dataset you already own, design a single template with four real modules, and ship fifty pages. Watch what the data does next.

#10291. Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air will be able to control Android XR glasses with gestures

Android XR glasses get their own control wearable, but you might already own it. Google says the Fitbit Air and Pixel Watch will be able to control Android XR glasses.

Google just launched its line of Intelligent Eyewear glasses. The smart glasses run on Android XR with hardware built by Samsung. They start at $395, with pre-orders opening up today through both Warby Parker and Gentle Monster.

For all the smart features they have, one of the best might be the sly integration Google is setting up between Intelligent Eyewear and its existing wearables. Google quietly noted that both Pixel Watch models and the Fitbit Air will be able to control Android XR glasses, like a dedicated control band some competitor smart glasses come with.

Pixel Watch already supports gesture controls using sensors to detect movements like finger pinching or wrist adjustments. Google’s Android XR demo included pausing and playing music using a pinch gesture. We imagine gesture controls will support as much as the touchpad that sits in the arms of Intelligent Eyewear models, though it’s not clear what gestures will be supported just yet. You’ll still be able to use the touch sensors, but Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air will be just another avenue.

According to what Google told us, the Pixel Watch will be supported around launch, with Fitbit Air adding support sometime in 2027. Galaxy Watch will also be supported on Samsung devices. Considering the Fitbit Air is reportedly such a massive hit that Google is having a hard time keeping up, the move only makes sense. The Fitbit Air is also only $99, whereas something like Meta’s Neural Band goes for $199.

Microsoft is starting to test new customization options for Xbox consoles today. Xbox Insiders will be able to start testing new rotating backgrounds, new library options for digital games, and the ability to move the Xbox Guide menu to the right-hand side.

“Xbox Insiders can now save favorite dynamic backgrounds and shuffle through them automatically,” says Eden Marie, principal software engineering lead at Xbox. “You can also choose Shuffle at the start of any background category. Have your background change each time you sign in, or set it to change hourly, daily, or weekly.”

Microsoft is also improving how you organize disc-to-digital games on an Xbox console. Xbox Insiders who have claimed a digital license from a disc-based game will be able to see these titles in a separate category in the library section. You’ll also be able to hide the digital license icon on game tiles.

Finally, Xbox Insiders will be able to choose whether the Guide menu appears on the left or right side of a screen. “Alpha Skip-Ahead members can try them first,” says Marie. “We’ll bring them to more Xbox Insiders and players over time.”

#10288. Developer creates a 100% valid ZIP file that doubles as a BIOS-bootable operating system

The original definition of "hacking" was making hardware and software do something it wasn't supposed to.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hacking stories these days mostly involve someone using an army of AI agents to find vulnerabilities in networks and software that were carefully hidden in plain sight. Today, for a change, we have a story about hacking in its original meaning — twisting computing artifacts into purposes not in their design spec. David Oberhollenzer, aka Goliath, a greybeard in spirit if not in shade, crafted a 100% valid ZIP file that's simultaneously an archive, BIOS-bootable, and a Multiboot-compatible OS image.

You can unpack the ZIP file just like any other, boot it using Linux's GRUB, QEMU, or any other Multiboot-compatible loader (even if imaged to different media), or alternatively, perform the finest party trick: image-write it to a floppy and boot from it directly from BIOS on any compatible PC, without modification.

Much like me, that sequence of words may be triggering a head-tilt reminiscent of a confused puppy, so we'll explain. The first thing you need to know is that unlike most file types, the metadata (in this case, file directory) of a ZIP is actually at the end of the archive. Up to that point, the data is comprised of every compressed file in sequence.

The file packs all have a predictable structure: "PK" followed by bytes 03 04, metadata (including name), some extra/comment fields, and then the file's contents. Notably, any file can be stored uncompressed, and Goliath made use of that for the very first file, called ZIPHEAD.COM — remember, this is the start of the ZIP archive.

Goliath said that it was "easy" to make the ZIP file a valid Multiboot system image. A valid image only needs to contain bytes 0x1BADB002 in the first 8 KiB, followed by some flags and a checksum. It was trivial to place that datum in ZIPHEAD's data, along with the location of where the bootloader lies.

Grub or QEMU don't care about anything else in the image, as once they have the initialization information, from that point it's the bootloader's problem. The actual bootloader, predictably, is in ZIPHEAD's uncompressed data, and the image will happily boot directly, or, say, from a floppy.

Making the same exact archive bootable via a standard PC BIOS took a little more work and some clever shenanigans. Making a disk bootable is simple enough: the BIOS reads the first exact 512 bytes of the disk's contents, and if they end with bytes 0x55AA, then it's bootable, and the BIOS starts executing whatever code is the first byte.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Placing 0x55AA precisely at position 510 of the ZIP, already in ZIPHEAD's contents, is once again simple, and won't corrupt the Multiboot image, as if you'll recall, the Multiboot spec is flexible with data locations. But you may wonder: once the BIOS runs code in sequence starting from the ZIP's first byte, then ZIPHEAD's header ("PK" + 0x0304, plus all metadata) surely is garbage for practical purposes, and not proper code? Well, yes, but actually no.

The first four bytes represent CPU instructions that don't write anything to memory, so they're harmless, other than a push-to-stack. The next few belong to ZIPHEAD's metadata, and Goliath massaged those values to represent instructions that would either be innocuous or perform operations he could use, namely using the modification time bytes as instructions to jump further down the data.

Sounds simple, but the actual process of choosing bytes that represent (1) non-crashy CPU instructions, (2) useful code for setting up the boot process, and (3) still represent a valid file header in a ZIP archive is far trickier than the above. Do go and check out the full blog post if you want all the details.

#10287. The price of your Android XR glasses can quickly skyrocket with add-ons

Google’s first collection of Intelligent Eyewear has arrived and, oh boy, the prices are not cheap. While this first batch starts at $395, you’ll be paying a fair bit more for a pair of Android XR glasses if you need to add anything on. Here’s a quick overview.

As we detailed earlier today, Android XR glasses from Warby Parker start at $395, with Gentle Monster’s collection starting at $449. And you’ll get a stellar pair for those prices, but add-ons will cost you.

For Warby Parker, we’ll focus on the “Reid” model, the rectangular lens shape. Like the rest of the catalog, these start at $395 with non-prescription lenses, either clear or tinted. It costs at least $100 to add prescription lenses, with other add-ons such as transition lenses or polarized lenses adding even further to the cost.

Here’s a breakdown of the potential add-ons for Warby Parker’s Android XR glasses:

Notably, there’s a further upcharge for adding prescription lenses to sunglasses versus standard clear glasses.

Just to get a pair of standard, non-prescription glasses with the addition of transition lenses, you’re looking at $520, and it can add up quickly in other configurations. One configuration we were able to build with progressive, polarized lenses with the “thinner lenses” and anti-reflective upgrades came out to $1,080.

Gentle Monster has a higher starting price for its Android XR glasses, but add-ons seem to be a bit more affordable based on what we’re seeing.

For this one, we’re looking at the GGS001 01 style which starts at $449.

Notably, Gentle Monster walks customers through potential insurance benefits even if they’re not buying prescription glasses.

I really want a pair. I'd be coming from meta oakley hstn's, but fit is partciular to my face. The hstn's were the only ones that felt comfortable for me of current "ai glass" offerings. I have a demo scheduled in a couple weeks at warby parker, but it'd be OON on my insurance too if anything.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Humble Bundle storefront for buying games and other software recently got a new look, but on the settings page, it no longer offers a way to add or update two-factor verification methods, as reported by Kotaku. PC Gamer also points out that there are Humble Bundle support pages discussing two-factor verification, and the writer for the publication said that they had set up two-factor verification on the old version of the site.

However, it does appear that, at least as of now, there is some form of two-factor verification. When I tried logging into my Humble Bundle account while writing this article, I was prompted to check my email for an access code before I could access my account. While logged in, I was able to update my password, but I didn’t have to enter my old password to be able to do so.

For what it’s worth, my passwords app also shows that I set up a rotating two-factor code for Humble Bundle at some point. I was not asked to input that at any part of my login process today.

Humble Bundle owner IGN Entertainment and IGN parent company Ziff Davis didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment.

#10285. Running WireGuard for a Development Team: The Hidden Cost of VPN Maintenance

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Setting up WireGuard on a small virtual server is a satisfying infrastructure task. Install the package, generate a key pair, open a UDP port, configure a peer, and connect a laptop. Within an afternoon, your staging environment can sit behind a private tunnel, and your internal dashboard can be restricted to traffic from a known address.

The initial setup is rarely the difficult part.

The real engineering work starts when the team changes. A developer joins, another leaves, someone replaces a laptop, a server needs patching, or a connection suddenly stops working. Each event creates a small maintenance task. Over time, those tasks become an operational responsibility that somebody must own.

This article examines what it takes to operate a self-hosted WireGuard VPN for a development team. We'll look at peer configuration, access management, troubleshooting, monitoring tools, and the engineering time involved. We'll also compare self-hosting with managed business VPN services so teams can make a decision based on their infrastructure needs.

WireGuard is a modern VPN protocol designed to establish encrypted connections between network peers. Its relatively small codebase, modern cryptography, and straightforward configuration make it attractive for developers who want control over their network infrastructure.

A basic WireGuard deployment typically involves:

A server with a reachable public IP address.

A private and public key pair for the server.

A UDP port through which peers communicate.

Peer configuration that defines which addresses are allowed through the tunnel.

A simplified server configuration might look like this:

The corresponding client configuration contains the client's private key and the server's public key:

These examples illustrate the basic relationship between a server and a client. A production configuration will also need appropriate firewall rules, routing, DNS settings, and a plan for distributing configuration securely.

The important distinction is that WireGuard establishes encrypted peer connections. It does not automatically provide a complete employee identity system, onboarding workflow, approval process, or audit dashboard.

Those responsibilities belong to the team operating the infrastructure.

Developers interested in using WireGuard on personal development devices can also explore SitePoint's guide to building a hardened mobile development environment with Termux, Docker, and WireGuard.

A peer is not just an entry in a configuration file. It represents a device that has permission to communicate through your network.

That makes peer management an access-control responsibility.

For a small development team, it is tempting to keep a single configuration file and update it manually whenever someone joins or leaves. This can work initially, but it becomes harder to manage as the number of devices increases.

A useful first step is to maintain a peer inventory:

The inventory should not contain private keys. Private keys must remain on their respective devices and should never be placed in a shared spreadsheet or source repository.

The inventory instead helps administrators answer practical questions:

Which device is associated with this tunnel address?

Has the peer been removed after the device was lost or replaced?

This information becomes especially important when a team uses the VPN to reach staging servers, internal dashboards, databases, or other development resources.

Manual onboarding is manageable when a team has only two or three developers. It becomes unreliable when several people join, leave, or change devices over the year.

A repeatable onboarding process should cover more than generating a key pair.

Confirm that the team member is authorized to access the environment.

Generate a key pair on the user's device.

Add the public key to the server's peer configuration.

Apply the appropriate routing and firewall permissions.

Deliver the client configuration through a secure channel.

Test access to the specific development resources the person needs.

Record the peer owner, device, and approval details.

Offboarding needs an equally clear process:

Identify every peer associated with the departing team member.

Remove the relevant public keys from the server.

Confirm that the configuration change has been applied.

Review any other credentials or allowlists that granted access.

An administrator can inspect the currently configured peers with:

The output helps identify peer public keys, transfer counters, and latest handshake information. It is useful for troubleshooting, but it should not be treated as a complete access audit or proof that a particular person is still authorized.

The broader lesson is to make access changes part of a documented workflow. A peer should not remain active simply because nobody remembers who created it.

One common network-design mistake is assuming that connecting to a VPN should give a developer access to every internal service.

A developer working on a frontend application may need access to a staging web server but not to the production database. A contractor may need access to one test environment without being able to reach internal administration tools.

WireGuard's peer configuration and the surrounding network controls should be designed around those requirements.

Separate tunnel address ranges for different groups.

Firewall rules that restrict traffic to approved services.

Cloud security groups that limit access to specific ports.

Application-level authentication for internal dashboards.

Separate production and staging network boundaries.

The exact implementation depends on the infrastructure. The key point is that VPN membership and application authorization are different controls.

A successful VPN handshake should not be treated as permission to access every resource behind the tunnel.

A VPN can appear simple when it works. Diagnosing it becomes more complicated when a developer is connecting from a home router, a hotel network, or a mobile hotspot.

Incorrect routing or AllowedIPs settings.

A server process or host becoming unavailable.

WireGuard provides useful inspection commands:

These commands help inspect peer information, check the service state, and review recent service logs. The exact systemd service name can differ depending on how the interface was configured.

Administrators should also monitor the underlying server. At minimum, that means checking:

Whether the WireGuard interface is active.

Whether disk or memory usage is approaching a limit.

Whether operating-system and package updates are pending.

A small virtual server may be inexpensive, but it is still infrastructure that needs an owner.

Teams working with alternative tunnel-based development workflows may find SitePoint's tutorial on setting up Cloudflare Tunnels with Docker Compose for persistent webhook debugging useful. It covers a different tunneling use case, but the operational questions around connectivity, service health, and troubleshooting are closely related.

A self-hosted VPN does not need to become a collection of manual tasks. A few established tools can make configuration, monitoring, and debugging more repeatable.

The core tool is, of course, WireGuard. Its command-line utilities allow administrators to inspect peers, manage interfaces, and review connection information. The official documentation is the best starting point for understanding configuration options and platform support.

Ansible can automate repeatable server administration tasks. A team could use playbooks to deploy configuration files, apply system updates, or standardize firewall settings across several VPN hosts.

Sensitive values should be handled with an appropriate secrets-management approach. Private keys should not be committed to a Git repository or stored in plaintext playbooks.

Prometheus is useful for collecting and querying metrics, while Node Exporter exposes Linux host metrics such as CPU, memory, disk, and network statistics.

These tools do not automatically provide a complete WireGuard security audit. They can, however, help identify resource problems and server-health issues before those problems become user-reported outages.

Uptime Kuma provides a self-hosted monitoring dashboard with notifications. It can help teams monitor the availability of a VPN host or related services.

A basic availability check does not prove that every VPN peer can connect successfully. For more meaningful monitoring, combine host checks with an end-to-end test from a separate network.

Wireshark and tcpdump help developers inspect network traffic when diagnosing connectivity problems.

For example, tcpdump can capture UDP traffic on the WireGuard port:

This can help determine whether packets are reaching the server. It does not decrypt WireGuard traffic or replace checks of routing, firewall rules, and peer configuration.

The right toolset depends on the size of the deployment. A team with one VPN server may only need WireGuard's own commands and basic host monitoring. A larger environment may benefit from automation, centralized metrics, and a documented incident process.

A self-hosted VPN can cost very little in direct infrastructure charges. However, the server invoice does not include the engineering time required to operate it.

Consider a hypothetical team with these assumptions:

Two people join and two leave during the year.

The team reviews or rotates device keys quarterly.

Patching and monitoring require about two hours per month.

Six troubleshooting incidents occur annually.

The estimated loaded engineering cost is $60 per hour.

The virtual server costs approximately $6 per month.

The following estimate illustrates how quickly maintenance time can exceed the infrastructure bill.

At $60 per hour, 46 hours represent approximately $2,760 in engineering time. The server adds around $72 per year, bringing the estimated total to $2,832.

These figures are illustrative, not a universal cost benchmark. A team with infrastructure automation and established monitoring may spend considerably less time. A team with frequent staff changes or strict audit requirements may spend more.

The useful exercise is to measure the work your own team performs. Track onboarding, access reviews, patching, incidents, and recovery time for a few months. That gives you a more realistic comparison than looking at hosting prices alone.

Self-hosting is not automatically the best option just because a team has developers who can configure servers.

A managed business VPN may be worth considering when the team needs centralized member management, simpler device administration, or a stable network exit address without maintaining the underlying VPN infrastructure.

Depending on the provider and plan, managed services may offer:

A central dashboard for adding and removing users.

Client applications for common operating systems.

Multi-factor authentication or identity integrations.

For example, PureVPN for Teams is one service in this category. Teams evaluating it—or any other provider—should check the current feature set, pricing, logging practices, access controls, and service terms before making a decision.

A managed service does not remove every security responsibility. The team still needs to decide who receives access, review permissions, protect user accounts, and understand how traffic and metadata are handled.

It also introduces vendor dependency and a recurring subscription cost. Those trade-offs should be considered alongside the engineering time saved.

There are situations where running WireGuard yourself is the more practical option.

You already operate infrastructure. If your team routinely patches servers, manages secrets, monitors services, and responds to incidents, maintaining a VPN may add relatively little work.

You need custom routing. A deployment that connects several private cloud networks, on-premises environments, or specialized subnets may require network controls that are easier to implement directly.

You have strict data-control requirements. Some organizations have policies that limit which third parties can handle network traffic or related metadata. Those requirements may favor infrastructure under the organization's own control.

You have a dedicated platform or security team. If someone is explicitly responsible for access management, monitoring, and incident response, self-hosting can fit naturally into existing operations.

You can automate the lifecycle. Infrastructure-as-code, configuration management, documented peer inventories, and tested recovery procedures can reduce repetitive work and make a self-hosted deployment easier to maintain.

The important distinction is between a deliberately operated service and a one-off server that nobody owns.

Before choosing an approach, ask the following questions:

Who is responsible for the VPN six or twelve months after deployment?

How many people and devices are likely to join or leave?

How are public keys and peer configurations tracked?

How quickly can access be revoked when a device is lost?

Which internal services should each developer be able to reach?

Do you need a fixed egress IP for external allowlists?

What monitoring and recovery process exists if the server fails?

Do your customers or internal policies require access logs or formal reviews?

Does your team have the time and expertise to maintain the infrastructure?

Would a managed service cost less than the engineering time and operational risk it replaces?

If your team already has mature infrastructure practices and needs custom network control, self-hosting may be a sensible choice.

If the team is small, access changes frequently, and nobody has time to own another server, a managed service may be more practical.

WireGuard is an excellent protocol, and deploying it can be a straightforward engineering task. But operating a VPN for a development team involves more than establishing an encrypted connection.

Peer management, onboarding, offboarding, network permissions, monitoring, patching, and troubleshooting all require ongoing attention.

The right choice depends on the team's infrastructure experience, security requirements, size, and available engineering time. Self-hosting offers control and flexibility; a managed service can reduce operational work. Neither option removes the need for sound access-control practices.

Before deciding, calculate the maintenance effort, identify who will own the service, and make sure the design can be maintained as the team changes.

The cost example uses illustrative assumptions rather than current vendor pricing. Managed VPN features and prices can change, so verify provider details before purchasing.

#10284. Hacker gets Doom running on a USB flash drive's built-in LCD, played by copying files

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Key presses turn into bursts of reads and writes to play the game.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Doom now runs on a USB flash drive’s built-in LCD and is played by copying files to and from it, as hardware hacker Aaron Christophel showed in a teardown video. The marking on the drive’s display chip had been removed, but an open debug port let him extract its firmware, which enabled identification. He was then able to use AI to reverse-engineer the hardware and get the game running on the drive’s screen.

The off-the-shelf drive, the MOVESPEED MSSU10, has USB-A on one end and USB-C on the other, and the 128GB model, the smallest capacity in the range, cost him about 50 euros. Its screen normally shows status data such as transfer speed, how full the drive is, and its temperature.

The main flash controller is the Silicon Motion SM2320G, one we have reviewed in portable SSDs like the Kingston XS2000 and Lexar SL660 Blaze, with a separate C*Core CCM4101 microcontroller — which has 2MB of flash and a small amount of RAM to itself — for the 428 x 142 color LCD. The NAND carries SanDisk markings. All of this is fairly standard portable SSD hardware, aside from the display.

The drive’s metal body uses heavily glued plastic end pieces but, past that, the display lifts off first and unplugs. The board slides out after the USB-C end cap is removed, in this case with minor cosmetic damage inflicted. The display chip’s marking is scrubbed, most likely with a laser, by the hacker’s reckoning, which can hinder identification and tinkering. Luckily, a probe on the debug pads, ground, and a 3.3V pin connected on the first try, and memory was immediately readable.

The bootloader returned a version string dated 2023, followed by the full firmware, which helped greatly with identification. He says the chip is marketed as one for security — for fingerprint readers and access control, for example — but no related features were found to be enabled for this drive. He also discovered that, although the data sheet and stock firmware declare a 200 MHz operating clock frequency, it measured 40.2 MHz. This is unexplained.

One critical issue is immediately apparent: how do you play on a drive with no buttons? The display chip has no USB connection of its own and, from the connected PC or host, it only sees the storage controller’s activity, which it uses to gauge transfer speed. The primary storage controller has read and write counters, and the chip pulls these every 50 ms over I2C. Christophel says this is far easier than relying on a single physical button, as he did on a previous power bank mod.

The solution, then, is a PC script, doomctl.py, which turns key presses into small, timed transfers in each direction. The commands are simply encoded as transfer rates, at five levels per direction, and each read/write pair can map to a certain button combination. Arrow keys are for movement and turning, Space or Ctrl for firing and opening doors, and M for the menu, keeping it simple. Ordinary copying, without the script, can still steer, but the control scheme is much nicer.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Performance is…well, not really playable at about 13 frames per second, although the game logic runs at Doom’s normal 35 ticks per second. This is why he names the chip’s clock as the biggest potential performance gain. The game itself is built on DMC100-DOOM, Artur Bieniek’s port for very small microcontrollers. Only the first map, E1M1, fits in the chip’s flash, and no sound is produced as the drive lacks a buzzer. Christophel did play the level end to end, noting the low resolution.

AI did the heavy lifting: the reverse engineering as well as the port, and Christophel said, “I would not be able to do such a thing in a few hours.”

The open-source project, with its code and prebuilt firmware published, is likely to draw attention and may spawn similar hacks. The tools required are relatively minimal, mainly a debug probe, and he does suggest backing up the stock firmware before trying. For his own unit, he put it back together to use as a normal drive, but one that can still play Doom, if so desired.

#10278. Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When Apple CEO John Ternus announced the first touchscreen Mac last month, the biggest reaction in the room wasn’t for the PROLED MacBook Pro’s OLED touchscreen or the thinner and lighter chassis; it was for the illuminated lid logo. It’s a throwback to the light-up logos on the old MacBooks, which Apple started phasing out in 2015, and which were completely gone by the 2018 MacBook Air redesign. It’s a nostalgia play, but boy did it work. It’s fun!

Just like in the old days, the new logo’s brightness is linked to the screen’s: The brighter or dimmer the screen, the brighter or dimmer the logo. But the light-up logo on the old MacBooks was illuminated by the screen’s backlight, and an OLED display doesn’t have one. Thanks to iFixit, we now know it’s lit by four white LEDs and synced with the screen’s brightness by software. But that means Apple could have used RGB LEDs.

Red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, violet, or anything in between? On the lid of your new MacBook Pro? Why not? After all, Apple insists it’s still serious about gaming, and nothing says “serious about gaming” like RGB lighting.

I was secretly hoping users would be able to customize the illuminated lid cutout much like the Xbox logo on Microsoft’s Elite Series 2 controllers. But alas, no luck. You can turn off the illumination with some Terminal commands or take control of it with a third-party app. But when it’s lit, it’s only lit with white light. And iFixit’s teardown also showed the difficulty of accessing those LEDs (the screen delaminated and broke in the process). So we’re unlikely to see LED RGB mods for it unless someone’s brave enough to void their warranty and risk a pricey screen repair on their $3,000 laptop.

The most fun you can have with the Apple lid logo is to install a free third-party app like LogoLight and have it pulse, breathe, match the beat of any music you’re playing, or flash some Morse code. There’s some fun there, but if you combined these features with a splash of color we’d really be cooking.

MacBooks aren’t gaming laptops, so RGB lighting may seem like a mismatch, but multicolor LEDs could have been a cool Easter egg. Nobody thought Apple would put a touchscreen on a MacBook either, and the iPhone Duo and HomeView also show Apple is in a new age of experimentation during the early Ternus era. Maybe if the light-up lid logo ever comes to future MacBook Airs or a MacBook Neo, Apple could cut loose a little.

#10276. Nvidia amassed $530 billion in financial guarantees to corner the AI market

Without a doubt, Nvidia's dominance in the AI hardware market stems not only from the capabilities of its AI accelerators and its vast software ecosystem, but also from its aggressive procurement strategy that effectively denies its rivals access to sufficient production capacity at TSMC and other contractors. The result of his deals is that supply and capacity commitments rose by almost a quarter of a trillion dollars in a single quarter, primarily due to memory procurement, according to SemiAnalysis. However, even Nvidia's former chief executive Jensen Huang had apparently begun to question the sustainability of this strategy before his departure, based on reports from The Wall Street Journal and SemiAnalysis.

Over the past several years, Nvidia has not only captured the vast majority of the AI accelerator market, but has also secured a substantial proportion of the world's production capacity for advanced logic, high-bandwidth memory (HBM), and various other components required to build AI accelerators and systems, which has limited the ability of its competitors to ramp up shipments of their processors. To support the deals, Jensen Huang personally negotiated agreements with major suppliers in Taiwan and South Korea.

The massive scale of Nvidia's procurement commitments also enabled the company to secure more favorable quotes than its rivals. SemiAnalysis estimates that SK hynix could charge Nvidia between $3.00 and $3.30 per gigabit of HBM4 memory in 2027, whereas other first-tier customers, such as AMD, Amazon, Broadcom, and Google, would pay between $3.70 and $4.10 per gigabit. The analysts believe that as part of an informal arrangement between the two companies, Nvidia gives SK Group preferential access to its AI GPUs.

While Nvidia's ability to secure vast production capacities has been instrumental in its success, such commitments also carry considerable risks. At a meeting with senior executives in August, Huang reportedly expressed concerns about the massive volumes of wafers and memory that Nvidia had committed to purchase as major cloud service providers sought to reduce their reliance on the company's hardware in favor of alternative AI accelerator platforms, according to The Wall Street Journal.

In a bid to diversify its customer base and create additional demand for its products, Nvidia offered financial guarantees to emerging cloud service providers and data center developers that otherwise could not afford to procure its hardware at scale. As of the end of its fiscal second quarter, Nvidia had $530 billion in gross off-balance-sheet guarantees, up from $184 billion a quarter earlier, as well as $99 billion worth of equity investments, according to SemiAnalysis. As half a trillion dollars seems gargantuan, here is a breakdown based on a filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission:

The scale of such commitments raised concerns even within Nvidia. Then-CFO Colette Kress questioned the criteria used to select recipients of financial support and the potential returns on such arrangements. She also reportedly reduced Nvidia's initial financial commitment to OpenAI's massive data center project in Ohio by half before the two companies signed an agreement on August 17. The facility is expected to house some $300 billion worth of Nvidia processors.

At some point, Huang himself began to question the merits of Nvidia's aggressive financing strategy in the event of his departure, essentially calling the current interim CEO, Colette Kress, a mere 'reporting CFO' rather than an executive capable of making strategic decisions. He also expressed concerns about massive wafer and memory procurement commitments as major cloud service providers sought to reduce their reliance on Nvidia’s GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Despite questionable commitments, without Huang and his personal relationships with executives at major semiconductor companies, Nvidia may find it harder to secure preferential treatment from its suppliers. SemiAnalysis believes that customers other than Nvidia could account for over 70% of TSMC production capacity in 2028. While this could enable Nvidia’s competitors to significantly increase shipments of their AI processors, it could also affect the willingness of Taiwanese and South Korean suppliers to invest aggressively in additional production capacity without the kind of assurances that Huang used to provide.

#10271. This tiny gamepad with dual analog thumbsticks pushes the limits of portable controllers

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

As much as I appreciate the ingenious design of the Nintendo Switch’s Joy-Con, my family and I can’t stand using them as standalone gamepads. So when we travel with the Switch 2, we always bring at least one full-size gamepad, plus a couple of 8BitDo’s Zero 2 controllers for games that don’t use analog thumbsticks. I’ve spent years hunting for an alternative that pairs the size of the Zero 2 with as much of the functionality of a larger controller as I can get, and CRKD’s new Atom Plus is nearly exactly that. But if you have larger hands like I do, you may find the gamepad’s focus on portability a struggle.

Measuring around 3.5 inches wide, the $29.99 Atom Plus is about a half-inch smaller than the 8BitDo Zero 2 but squeezes in considerably more features and functionality. The most obvious is a pair of symmetrical analog thumbsticks with anti-drift tunneling magnetoresistance technology. You also get shoulder buttons and triggers, rumble feedback, a dedicated home button, motion controls, and customization through a mobile app.

Like many third-party controllers, the Atom Plus works with multiple platforms, including the Switch and Switch 2, PCs, Android, iOS, and various smart TVs. And just like other gamepads, pairing to different devices requires putting the Atom Plus into one of several Bluetooth modes by holding down a combination of buttons at power-up, which in this case is the home button plus a specific direction on the D-pad. It’s easy to do, but never easy to remember: I frequently needed to grab the manual or quickly Google which direction I need to press when switching devices. The Atom Plus already features a bunch of technical info etched into the back of the controller, so I’d love to see these important pairing instructions included there, too. (And that goes for all multiplatform gamepads.)

I primarily tested the Atom Plus with the Nintendo Switch 2 since I mostly plan to use the controller as a travel accessory. The thumbsticks are about the same size as those on the original Switch Joy-Con, which means they’re smaller than the Switch 2’s. And while I prefer an asymmetrical layout, the Atom Plus’ sticks are very good, with a little more tension than Nintendo’s that I find actually helps with precision movements given their limited travel.

While the overall build quality feels excellent, the Atom Plus’ tiny footprint creates some compromises and challenges. The front action buttons and D-pad feel nice and squishy, but the gamepad’s controls are a little crowded. When pressing down or right on the D-pad, my thumb regularly bumps the left thumbstick. It doesn’t hinder gameplay, but I do find it an annoying distraction.

The shoulder buttons use digital microswitches, as do the triggers, which means the latter can’t be used for racing games that support analog acceleration controls. I like that the two sets of buttons on the top edge sit at slightly different heights, but I would have preferred a little more differentiation since I’ve occasionally accidentally pressed the triggers when I meant to hit the shoulder buttons.

I was impressed with the Atom Plus’ customizability, however. When paired to an accompanying mobile app, you can check the controller’s remaining charge, turn off rumble feedback, see its unique product number (by tapping your phone to an NFC chip inside the controller), and remap any of its controls. That includes the pair of dual-function shortcut levers that replicate the Switch’s plus and minus buttons and screenshot shortcut.

Those levers move up and down and cleverly reduce the number of buttons crowding the front of the Atom Plus, but they feel a little too small, which is more or less the general theme. It’s a tiny gamepad that’s great for travel or stashing in your everyday carry. But, unsurprisingly, it probably won’t find its way into your regular gaming rotation. It’s assumed that players will be willing to settle for the compromise of occasionally contorting their fingers in weird, new ways for one of the smallest fully featured controllers on the market.

I don’t think a dual-stick controller could get any smaller than the Atom Plus without making too many ergonomic compromises. So while I applaud CRKD for the careful balancing act behind the Atom Plus’ design, and would certainly recommend it for those wanting a very capable controller that’s also cheap and portable, I’m now curious how it compares to Microsoft’s recently announced Xbox Nano controller that’s “surprisingly comfortable,” according to The Verge’s Tom Warren. It’s $20 more expensive than CRKD’s and noticeably larger, but it adds analog triggers, a more spacious design, and stubby grips making it easier to hold for those of us with beefy mitts.

#10268. The Design Workflow Is Broken. Here Is How Vitalii Khomenko Thinks It Should Be Fixed

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

With 250+ client engagements across both agency and embedded in-house roles, Vitalii Khomenko has seen product teams from the inside in a way few designers have. Here he makes the case for a new kind of design workflow built for the AI era, and explains why the window to adapt is narrower than most founders think.

For the last decade, every startup conversation about speed eventually became a conversation about engineering. Hiring more developers, cutting scope, choosing a simpler stack, it was almost always a dev-side problem. Vitalii Khomenko, Principal Designer at Orizon Design and founder of Fluente LLC, argues that's no longer true. And he has a specific view on what teams should do instead.

What makes Khomenko's perspective worth taking seriously isn't just seniority. It's the specific shape of his experience. Over a career spanning 250+ client engagements, he has worked both as an external specialist brought in to deliver and as an embedded designer who became the single connective thread between product, brand, and execution. That dual vantage point is rare. Most designers live on one side of that line. Khomenko has operated extensively on both, which gives him an unusually clear read on where friction actually originates inside product teams, and where it doesn't.

His work spans early-stage startups finding their first visual language and established brands managing complex, multi-disciplinary design systems. Across that range, certain patterns repeat. And the pattern that started becoming impossible to ignore this year pointed him toward a conclusion that is reshaping how he thinks a modern design workflow should actually be structured.

The bottleneck didn't disappear. It moved. And the workflow needs to move with it.

When shipping code was slow, the existing design workflow made sense. Multiple rounds of revision, user testing, iteration, all of that fit naturally into the window while engineering caught up. That window is now gone, and most design workflows were never updated to reflect it.

"Founders are asking design teams to move at the same speed as an AI-assisted dev pipeline, and design doesn't compress the same way code does," Khomenko says. The result is a workflow that was built around a constraint that no longer exists, applied to a pipeline that has fundamentally changed around it.

The forced choice between polish and speed that product teams are now navigating isn't new as a concept. What's new is how stark it has become, and how directly it traces back to a workflow problem rather than a people problem. "Because development is cheap and fast now, there's constant pressure to ship the next thing immediately," he explains. "But the products that actually retain users are still the ones where the details were considered carefully. Fewer revision rounds isn't a process improvement if it means shipping avoidable friction."

Khomenko has watched this play out across enough teams, at enough stages, to be specific about where the existing workflow breaks down. The issue isn't design judgment. It's where that judgment sits in the process, and what format it's expected to produce at the end.

To understand Khomenko's proposed fix, it helps to understand exactly where the current workflow stalls. For most product teams, the end of the design process looks like this: a designer produces polished, dev-ready screens inside Figma, and hands that file to engineering. Engineering then interprets those screens, translates the visual decisions into code, and builds from there.

That translation step, Figma file to working code, is where the pipeline now breaks down. AI-assisted engineering has made the building part dramatically faster. But the translation step hasn't changed. An engineer still has to read a Figma file, interpret spacing, extract assets, make judgment calls about component structure, and write the implementation from scratch. That process takes the same amount of time it always did.

"The manual workflow inside Figma simply isn't keeping up with AI-boosted engineering," Khomenko says. "The design handoff is quietly becoming the thing everyone is waiting on."

The result is a pipeline where everything around the handoff has accelerated, and the handoff itself has become the single point of drag. Figma, in other words, was built for a world where engineering was the slow part. It wasn't designed to feed a pipeline where engineering moves at AI speed.

This is the core of what Khomenko is proposing, and it's a more significant structural shift than it might first appear.

In his workflow, Figma doesn't disappear. It moves. Rather than serving as both the exploration environment and the final delivery artifact, Figma becomes the visual exploration layer exclusively: the place where brand identity is established, layout logic is worked through, and design direction is validated. It remains the right tool for figuring out what something should look and feel like.

But the final delivery, the artifact that actually goes to engineering, is code. Specifically, components built into prepared libraries that engineering can work from and scale directly, without any translation step in between.

The difference in implementation speed is the point. When a designer delivers a coded component library rather than a Figma file, engineering doesn't need to interpret anything. The decisions are already expressed in the language they're building in. What previously required an engineer to read, interpret, and re-create from a visual reference now arrives ready to use. In a pipeline running on AI-assisted development, that difference compounds fast.

"Figma is still the right place to explore what something should look and feel like," Khomenko says. "But handing over a Figma file and asking engineering to implement it is now the slow path. Delivering in code is the fast path. That's the shift."

Executing this workflow requires a different kind of designer, and Khomenko is direct about that. The UI/UX designer becoming a Design Engineer is, in his assessment, not a distant possibility. It is a matter of time, and the teams treating it as a future consideration rather than a present reality are the ones most likely to find themselves stuck at the handoff.

But Khomenko is equally clear about what this shift doesn't mean. Delivering in code is one part of the role, not the whole of it. The full design cycle isn't going anywhere. Applying branding to imagery, producing animations, building marketing materials, these remain essential creative tasks that require a broad and varied toolkit well beyond code. "The designer of the near future isn't just generating code," he says. "They're moving across the full cycle, brand, motion, marketing, product, with design engineering as an important and growing part of that stack, not a replacement for it."

What changes is that code-ready delivery becomes a core expectation within that stack. The designer who can move fluidly from visual exploration in Figma to component delivery in code, while also carrying the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience, is the profile that the new workflow demands.

Khomenko is candid that no single version of this workflow has fully standardized yet. Every team is figuring it out differently. The tools are evolving, the handoff expectations are shifting, and the processes teams are building around all of it vary considerably. There is no off-the-shelf playbook.

But this is precisely where his 250+ engagements become more than a credential. They're a dataset. Having watched enough teams, at enough stages, through enough cycles of tooling change, Khomenko can see through the noise of individual variation and identify what's directionally true even when the specifics are still in flux. "Each team's workflow is really custom at this stage," he says. "But some general patterns of how this is going to look in the near future are already visible."

Those patterns consistently point in the same direction: design judgment moving earlier, final delivery moving into code, and the designer's role expanding in technical range while staying broad in creative scope. The exact tools and sequences will keep shifting. The underlying logic of the workflow Khomenko is describing is already taking shape in the teams paying closest attention.

For founders and product leads, the workflow Khomenko is describing has direct implications for how teams are staffed, budgeted, and structured.

Front-load design judgment. The biggest leverage in the new workflow isn't faster execution, it's better problem definition before generation starts. Bring senior design thinking in earlier, when it can shape what gets built rather than polish what's already done.

Separate exploration from delivery. Figma and the visual exploration phase serve a distinct purpose from the final delivery layer. Treat them as separate stages with separate expectations, rather than collapsing them into a single step expected to produce everything.

Shift the delivery expectation to code. Whether that means hiring for Design Engineer range, developing it internally, or restructuring handoffs, the end of the design pipeline needs to arrive in a form engineering can work from directly. The translation step is where time goes to die, and it's now the most avoidable part of the process.

Protect the full creative stack. Design engineering matters more than it ever has. It is not, however, the whole job. Teams that reduce design to code generation will lose the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience.

Stay flexible. The workflow is still forming. Teams that build rigidity into their process now will find themselves redoing that work within a year. The teams getting this right are the ones building enough adaptability into their structures to absorb what's still coming.

"The Figma handoff made sense when engineering was the slow part," Khomenko says. "Engineering isn't the slow part anymore. Delivering in code isn't the future of design workflow. For the teams paying attention, it's already the present."

I write about AI and software development from the angle that usually gets skipped: what a spec sheet or benchmark actually implies once you try to build with it. Find me on LinkedIn.

#10264. Fable 5.2 creates an underwater world in a browser in only 96 KB

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Elegant effort is reminiscent of both old- and new-school demoscene.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Claude Fable 5.2 was released recently to mostly rave reviews, with both users and analysis outlets ranking it nipping at the heels of the much pricier Opus 5.5. Perhaps as practical proof of that claim, front-end developer Maksymilian Dendura took inspiration from the demoscene and had Fable roll his own virtual underwater world, Abyssal Garden, in only 96 KB of Javascript/HTML — you can check it out here.

The demo's 3D space and textures are all procedurally generated, meaning they're the result of mathematical equations rather than prebuilt 3D meshes, and all calculated on load. Using actual meshes and images for textures would otherwise take up a significant amount of space.

Building structures procedurally is difficult and has limitations, but it's exceedingly effective when used properly. Some games employ this technique for repeated elements like trees and grass.

Likewise, all the visual effects and even some texturing are done with shaders, which are small programs that change how a mesh or texture appears — for example, to make parts of an object shiny. Games use them extensively, as many players are aware, thanks to endless "loading shaders" screens or Unreal Engine's st-tu-tutering.

The result is definitely impressive, especially but not only as it's purely browser-based, relying on WebGPU and WebGL. Both are rarely-mentioned technologies that have actually existed for a long while now. As their names imply, they let developers render 2D and 3D graphics in a manner similar to that of OpenGL, but in a browser, using JavaScript as the base language.

The demo's most impressive fact is the simplicity of the prompt that got Fable 5.2 to build it. It's only about 40 lines long with word wrapping, and clearly illustrates the degree of autonomy that contemporary agentic AI has when given a specific, yet exceedingly open-ended task like this one. It's reasonable enough to assume the author did a few rounds of tweaking (and he has excellent artistic taste too), but the agent's acumen is nevertheless impressive.

Comparing this demo to an actual game is inevitable, but the reality is that demos have but an atom-sized fraction of the content of a full game, and are limited to little or no interaction, logic, or physics.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Performance is decent for what is a great-looking but otherwise tiny and almost featureless space. The author says that he's getting 180 FPS at 1440p on an RTX 5070 Ti. Likewise, having this world in 97 KB sounds impressive, but it's actually gigantic for this type of demo, even if an X user further optimized it for 52 KB.

Regular computing users, including AI neophytes and acolytes, may not be aware that this kind of space-optimized programming for the purposes of visual/aural demonstrations is a discipline and art form that harkens back to the 1980s when 16-bit computers appeared — called the "demoscene."

By no means discounting the extraordinary fact that Fable did this nearly all by itself — which is the actual achievement here — code wizards have pulled off nearly impossible feats decades ago, including in browsers. The 2009 demo Elevatedn (17 years ago) has a comparable aesthetic, and includes music, in just under 4 KB (yes, four). There's even a browser version of it, taking up all of 4,777 bytes.

Hartverdrahtet (2012 — 14 years ago) has a highly complex 3D space, again in 4 KB. And old-timers in the audience will remember ".kkrieger", an FPS game prototype that fit in all of 96 KB, including meshes, textures, music, and sound effects... back in 2004.

#10262. 7 Best Accounts Receivable (AR) Software Platforms for Automating Invoice-to-Cash

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best accounts receivable software for your company is the layer that closes the stage where your cash leaks. If buyers settle from an emailed link and outreach starts late, a reminders tool covers most of it. If customers prefer to pay online by card or ACH, add a payments network with a customer portal. If clients require invoice uploads to Coupa or Ariba and clear multiple invoices with one blank-memo wire, you need an AR automation layer on top of the ERP. Every platform listed here serves B2B businesses invoicing other companies, excluding consumer payment apps and collection agencies.

Our source material is each vendor's public product, integration, security and pricing pages, plus G2 reviews, as they stood in November 2026.

About three in five US suppliers let business clients pay within 30 days, per Atradius's 2026 US payment practices survey (September 2026). Most overdue invoices settle within a month past due, so funds arrive roughly two months post-billing. The same survey notes US enterprises often lose 1% to 2% of B2B invoices to bad debt. Software that shortens this window performs four main functions:

What invoice-to-cash software does not handle is core billing operations. Billing schedules, revenue recognition, and GL accounting remain within the ERP. Contract-first platforms initiate workflows from signed agreements and map customer billing prior to collection efforts; invoice-first applications pick up after ERP invoice generation.

Most finance stacks have four layers: the ERP that creates the invoice and holds the books, a payments network, a reminders tool, and an AR automation layer working the steps in between.

The ERP covers the last row, and a reminders tool covers timing. The rows in between depend on reading what a customer or a portal sent, which is AR automation work. That share keeps growing. Paper checks fell to 26% of B2B payments in 2025, per the AFP 2025 Digital Payments Survey as reported by Nacha, and FedNow settled about 2.5 times as many payments in Q3 2026 as a year earlier, per Federal Reserve FedNow statistics. Heading into 2027, more money arrives over electronic rails while its remittance detail lands somewhere else.

Layer: AI-native AR automation, for Series A to Fortune 500 finance teams

Published pricing: Pricing scales with business volume and isn't a percentage of collections

Leak it closes: Unapplied cash first, then stalled replies and portal rejections.

Layer: AR automation with built-in payments, sold as Invoiced by Flywire

Leak it closes: Cash application, where CashMatch AI handles wires that pay several invoices at once and payments that arrive without remittance.

Leak it closes: Invoice delivery into buyer portals, through 260+ AP portal integrations run from one interface.

Layer: AR automation layer with AI agents, on the ERP

Leak it closes: Customer replies that carry a promise-to-pay date or a dispute.

Layer: Payments network with customer portal

Leak it closes: Payment acceptance, through a customer portal for paying and disputing invoices online.

Layer: Reminders tool with AI reply drafts, on Xero and QuickBooks

Published pricing: Essentials $69 a month; Pro $99 to $799 a month; Custom from $999 a month

Leak it closes: Replies that sit unanswered at small and midsize companies.

Published pricing: US $259, $779 or $1,169 a month by annual revenue band; annual billing saves 10%

Leak it closes: Follow-up timing, with reminders sent from your own email address on a schedule that escalates.

On Xero or QuickBooks with customers paying from the emailed link, the leak tends to be timing, and a reminders tool covers it. Once W-9 requests and payment questions fill your team's week, the replies need a different layer.

Portal rejections and lump-sum wires put the leak in the table's middle rows, where an AR automation layer on the ERP fits. In a demo, we'd ask each vendor to show how fast its software flagged a rejected portal invoice, then to apply a multi-invoice wire with a blank memo live.

If your team still keys card numbers or chases ACH details, start with the payments layer. A payments network with a customer portal gives buyers stored methods and autopay. Unless the vendor says otherwise, it won't read replies or file into buyer portals, so plan an AR layer for those stages.

Whichever of the best accounts receivable software platforms you pick, it sits on top of the ERP. Four steps keep the books intact:

#10255. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10254. I’d rather use Android XR glasses over Meta Ray-Bans for the app alone

In trying out Google’s Intelligent Eyewear Android XR glasses for the first time, one of my main takeaways is that it’s the app, not necessarily the glasses themselves that would push me into using them over the mainstay Meta Ray-Bans.

One of the inherent advantages that Android XR glasses were always going to have over third-party options is native integration. It’s something we see frequently, and perhaps most well-known on smartwatches.

The “Glasses” app on Android/Pixel, iOS, and Samsung Galaxy devices is dead simple. While we can’t show you the full app just yet as the one we were able to try was not completely final, it’s a very simple experience.

The app opens to an overview of your glasses to show their connection and battery status, with settings below for Gemini, apps, the camera, and more. You’ll also be able to see things such as what the various lights on the glasses mean, tutorials, and more. There’s no bloat and no messing around – it’s a utility to just make adjustments or control the glasses as needed.

You’ll probably be using this app mostly to view the photos and videos captured on Android XR glasses. Google has this set up to auto-import photos in nearly real-time. It only takes about 4 seconds to import and the process starts immediately for photos. Videos, on the other hand, take longer and may need to be manually imported (for the sake of battery in part, Google mentioned).

Across the three main platforms, there are some differences. The iPhone app doesn’t import photos quite as fast, and Samsung has its own app to fit in with One UI’s design language for Galaxy users, but the core premise is the same across all three.

While we’ll have to see how this works in real life, I was blown away by how simple it all was, though that might be because the bar is below the floor.

Meta’s Ray-Bans (and newer Meta Glasses) dominate the smart glasses conversation, and I think that the app is one of the biggest problems. It’s certainly one of the biggest reasons I’ve barely touched my Wayfarers in recent months. That’s because, in early 2025, Meta replaced its dedicated “View” app for its glasses with a Meta AI app, which is just invasive and distasteful in my book. Meta is no stranger to shoving AI down the throat of its existing users. Another well-known move was converting Facebook and Instagram search into AI search boxes with creepy levels of context and invasive actions – it makes Google’s AI Overviews look tame by comparison. But I’d argue the most aggressive was this move with its glasses. Something as simple as tweaking a setting or importing a photo now forces you to dodge an AI chatbot.

Truly, Meta’s app is the worst, and I applaud Google for building a very simple utility for Android XR glasses instead.

Just days after he left the company under a cloud of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang might be on his way back to the company he founded.
