After placing chief financial officer Colette Kress in charge on an interim basis, it's believed the company has approached Arm chief executive Rene Haas as a potential Huang successor. Haas spent around seven years at Nvidia before leaving in 2013, but no official confirmation has been announced, nor is it likely to be until a decision is made.

It's possible that Huang can expect a vote of confidence from U.S. President Donald Trump, with the pair having a good relationship to date. But Trump recently called for former Apple CEO Tim Cook to take over at Nvidia.

Outside of the White House, Huang has other concerns to deal with. Congress has summoned him to appear before a Select Committee over his affair with the suspected Chinese spy, threatening to subpoena him if necessary.

#10251. This Quest 3 golf club actually improved my scores, but it comes at a cost

The AMVR GA2 is a fresh take on a Quest 3 golf club accessory, and I was pleasantly surprised with what it did for my scores.

In the past decade of covering VR devices and accessories, I’ve used a lot of golf clubs. Short clubs. Long clubs. Adjustable length clubs. Clubs with realistic weight. Even a club with a magnet inside that makes a convincing “thwack!” sound every time you swing it. But the AMVR GA2’s inclusion of a proper club head at the bottom does something fascinating for its physics, and I immediately noticed the difference at the end of the first course I played with this Quest 3 golf club.

Most VR golf club accessories, including the first-gen AMVR GA1, stick the controller at the bottom of the shaft where the head usually goes. This makes sense since you don’t hit a real ball with a VR golf club accessory; it’s all virtual. But, ironically, putting a proper golf club head on the end (and the controller in the middle of the shaft) apparently does make a difference. This one works with both the Meta Quest 3 and Quest 3S, but not the older Meta Quest 2 or Quest Pro.

I’m not going to delve into the physics of why this works, but it’s likely that shifting the fulcrum point improves it. I didn’t expect this at all. When AMVR told me about their new club, I accepted because the company typically makes top-notch accessories. I really didn’t need another club in my extensive Quest 3 golf club collection. But I’m glad I did, because I’ve done much better than usual in my weekly Walkabout Mini Golf rounds with friends since I got it.

The mechanism for securing the controller onto the club is identical to previous AMVR controllers; only the location of the mount is different. Slide the controller (with wrist strap attached) into the holster. Click the rubber stopper in so the controller doesn’t move, then loop the wrist strap around the controller as a backup in case you didn’t secure the rubber stopper properly. It’s quite simple.

All major VR golf games allow custom spacing for Quest 3 golf clubs, so it doesn’t take more than 30 seconds to get comfortable with the club in your game of choice.

The only problem I’ve found with this club is its price. Most Quest 3 golf club accessories cost between $30 and $50, depending on the capabilities. This one is normally double that, but AMVR was kind enough to pass along a 25% off coupon for 9to5Google readers, making it more palatable if you’re looking to improve your VR golf scores. Just apply the coupon code 1FD4JYT5 at checkout on Amazon for an extra 25% off the AMVR GA2 golf club.

If you’re serious about your scores in Golf+, Walkabout Mini Golf, or another favorite of yours, this might just be the ultimate enthusiast Quest 3 golf club accessory.

Time to re-install mid-quarter updates at Intel?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel has been sued by shareholders who accuse the company of misleading investors about the severity of the PC market downturn and its impact on the company's financial performance before its shares plunged by nearly 29% in late October, reports Reuters.

The proposed class action lawsuit led by the Southfield Fire and Police Retirement System pension fund was filed on Friday in a federal court in San Jose, California. The lawsuit alleges that Intel failed to inform investors in a timely manner about the extent to which slow demand for consumer processors and excessive inventories would affect its sales. In particular, the plaintiffs argue that the negative impact of the PC market downturn on Intel's business was greater than the benefits the company enjoyed from the unprecedented buildout of AI data center infrastructure and other AI-related opportunities.

"Intel made statements that were materially false and/or misleading when made because they failed to disclose the following adverse facts pertaining to Intel’s business," the lawsuit reads.

While Intel, like many other major technology companies, has invested heavily in its manufacturing capabilities, supply constraints have prevented the chipmaker from fully capitalizing on strong demand for data center processors. Meanwhile, a severe shortage of memory has driven up DRAM prices and, consequently, the cost of PCs, which has affected demand for consumer systems. As a result, Intel could not sell enough CPUs in the third quarter, which dramatically affected the company's stock price in October.

Intel chief executive Lip-Bu Tan expressed disappointment with the supply constraints, the extraordinary situation in the memory market, and the subsequent weakness of the PC business during the company's earnings call. Yet, the plaintiff believes that the company should have warned about the turmoil situation on the client PC market earlier. In fact, back in the early 2000s, when Intel was led by Craig Barrett, the company routinely issued mid-quarter business updates to inform investors about changes in its financial outlook. In fact, in September 2004, Intel used such an update to cut its third-quarter revenue and gross margin projections due to weaker-than-expected demand for its processors and inventory adjustments by its customers, which were very similar circumstances that the company faced in the third quarter of 2026.

A similar update could have alerted Intel's shareholders to the challenges the chipmaker faced well ahead of its quarterly earnings release. Unfortunately, Intel abandoned the practice of mid-quarter updates in January 2006 under Paul Otellini, though it occasionally revised its guidance between quarterly earnings releases thereafter, particularly during the global financial crisis of 2008 – 2010.

Intel's shareholders also allege that the company had a financial incentive to withhold information about the state of its business as it raised $23 billion through a stock offering at $95 per share in mid-August, roughly halfway through its third quarter. Intel secured the additional capital as it planned to expand its semiconductor production capacity.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel’s stock price dropped by 28.6% on October 30, erasing around $140 billion of the company's market capitalization, after the chipmaker issued Q4 revenue and earnings projections that fell short of expectations and warned that the PC market downturn would persist. Meanwhile, despite the massive sell-off, Intel's shares have gained 62% since the beginning of the year.

The lawsuit seeks unspecified damages on behalf of Intel investors who purchased the company's shares between July 23 and October 29, 2026. Intel CEO Lip-Bu Tan and CFO David Zinsner are also named as defendants.

#10247. What is Model Routing in AI?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

#10246. Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

Take a look at Proton’s Black Friday offers, starting at $1.99 per month

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

It needs no PC and no internet, just a console running GoldHEN.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Developer Leonardo Alegría’s open-source project Garlic LLM lets a jailbroken PS4 run the 9-billion-parameter Qwen3.5-9B AI model offline on the console’s 2013-era GPU. The app talks to the GPU directly, and the model is shrunk to about 4.5GiB to fit in the console’s memory, running at 31 to 39 tokens per second.

The project, named after the memory the GPU reads directly — its sibling memory type is “Onion” — relies on a PS4 running GoldHEN, which is a homebrew enabler for exploited consoles. Garlic LLM is a free, single 5.66GB download, available on Hugging Face and installable through the PS4’s Package Installer, with about 6GB free needed, or 12GB if the package is first copied to the console’s internal disk. The developer tested it on a base/Slim PS4 on firmware 11.52.

It’s capable of voice conversation, with spoken replies in seven languages through the Chatterbox speech model and the chat model running at about 22 tokens per second. A held controller button also lets it take dictation with Nvidia’s Parakeet TDT 0.6B v3 speech-to-text model, which supports 25 European languages. Parakeet models also power local transcription and dictation apps such as Meetily and Hex. The main model is Alibaba’s small open-weight Qwen3.5-9B, which, at the claimed 31 to 39 tokens per second in text chat — the prompt and test method are not given — is usable on the console.

The closest comparison is the PS5LM fork, which runs llama.cpp as a native app on the PS5 and reaches the console’s AMD GPU through PS5_Vulkan, a port of the RADV driver. The open-source llama.cpp is a widely used program for running language models locally, and RADV is the open-source driver that lets AMD GPUs run Vulkan, the graphics and compute API. On the PS4 there is no such driver layer for homebrew; Garlic LLM uses neither llama.cpp nor a Vulkan port, or so it would appear.

The model’s operations, such as matrix multiplies over 4-bit weights, are compute kernels written in OpenCL C and compiled with LLVM for AMD’s Graphics Core Next (GCN) architecture. The weights are kept in that “Garlic” memory, while the CPU side, in plain C, handles the tokenizer, the interface, text rendering, and audio. The pipeline runs from the prompt to the CPU tokenizer, then, for each token, a PM4 command buffer — the low-level commands a graphics driver would normally generate, which, in this case, the app builds itself — and the GCN kernels, before the token is displayed or spoken. The whole thing is built with the open-source OpenOrbis PS4 toolchain.

One immediate question is how you fit a 9-billion-parameter model in the console’s 8GB of memory, which the CPU and GPU share. At 8 bits per weight, you have one byte per parameter, meaning you’d need at least 9GB, with typically more than that required overall. Part of the answer is 4-bit (“Q4”) weights, which take half as much, although 3-bit (“Q3”) weights, at about 3.5 bits with scales, are used for the two largest matrices in each layer.

Using the combination gets you to about 4.5GiB, versus about 4.9GiB if it were all 4-bit. The attention, or KV, cache is also 4-bit, which lets a 16K-token context fit, in comparison to the model’s native 256K. The Parakeet dictation model adds 0.33GiB and the Chatterbox voice models 0.29GiB and 0.09GiB, for a grand total of 5.23GiB, or 5.62GB, which closely matches the 5.66GB package size.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The next question is performance. Qwen3.5 comes with a multi-token prediction (MTP) head, and Garlic LLM uses it to draft up to two tokens ahead, with the full model verifying. This matches what the model was trained for, and the vLLM instructions on Alibaba’s model card use two speculative tokens. The project credits this for its chat speed on 2013 hardware.

Code also comes out faster with this setup, at up to 43 tokens per second. Also detailed is the default temperature, or model variability, which is 0.7 — Alibaba recommends 0.7 for general tasks with thinking off and 1.0 with it on — although the demo video uses 0, so it is predictable. At 35 tokens per second, the middle of the reported performance range, a 300-token answer takes under nine seconds. As a comparison, the PS5 ran the three-times-larger Qwen3.8-27B at about 20 tokens per second and the smaller Gemma 4 E4B and Llama 3.2 3B at 61 and 124, respectively, which is potentially quicker.

The PS4 Pro is “not tested yet,” but the developer is asking users for reports on GitHub. The PS5 is not supported as the app is written specifically for the PS4’s GPU and system software. The app itself contains no exploit, but GoldHEN is installed at the user’s own risk.

#10239. How to Migrate an Elementor Site to the WordPress Block Editor

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Elementor made your site easy to build. It may also be making it slow. Here's how to move a site onto the Block Editor without breaking URLs, losing content or dropping out of Google.

Page builders solved a real problem. Before the Block Editor matured, they were the easiest way to give clients drag-and-drop layouts. Many of those sites are still running today, and a lot of them are slow on phones.

The data backs that up. According to the HTTP Archive Core Web Vitals Technology Report for June 2026, 36.8% of Elementor sites pass Core Web Vitals on mobile. Sites built with the Block Editor pass 53.0% of the time. That's correlation, not proof, since hosting and setup matter too. But in my experience, it matches what happens when you remove the builder from a slow site.

My reason for migrating sites off Elementor is simple: **page builders make websites heavy. **Having said that, Elementor has improved a lot. Its performance settings, Flexbox Containers, and asset-loading options cut much of the bloat, and a carefully tuned Elementor site can pass Core Web Vitals. But tuning only goes so far. You’re still shipping the builder’s framework, wrapper markup and scripts, and every plugin or Elementor update is a chance for the speed to slip again.

For small business sites that don’t need drag-and-drop editing, rebuilding the same design on the WordPress Block Editor (Gutenberg) removes that weight at the source, without changing what the client sees.

This guide follows the order I use. It assumes you're comfortable with WordPress admin, WP-CLI and a staging site. If you need to set those up first, SitePoint's guide to the ultimate WordPress development environment covers the tooling.

Not every Elementor site needs to move. Migration makes sense when:

It's harder to justify when the site leans on Elementor Pro features you'd need to replace, such as complex popups, dynamic loops or WooCommerce templates. Those can all be rebuilt, but the job gets bigger fast.

You can't prove a migration worked without "before" numbers. Capture these on the live site first.

Crawl the site with a tool such as Screaming Frog, and export every indexable URL with its title, meta description, H1 and canonical tag. You can also pull published pages and posts with WP-CLI:

Run Google PageSpeed Insights, which uses Lighthouse for its lab scores, on the homepage and two or three key templates. Save the mobile lab scores and, if the site has enough traffic, the field data from real users.

Export the last three months of pages and queries from Google Search Console. That's what you'll compare against after launch.

Elementor stores each page's layout as JSON in the elementordata post meta field, and marks builder pages with elementoredit_mode. This command lists every page that uses the builder:

Then list everything else Elementor controls, which is easy to miss:

On the small business sites I've migrated, the inventory usually turns up more than the owner expects. The pages are the easy part. The surprises are saved templates nobody remembers creating, a popup still running on every page, add-on widgets from plugins that were installed once and forgotten, and contact forms that live inside Elementor Pro rather than a form plugin. Finding those before the rebuild saves a broken launch.

Work on a staging copy. Install a block theme, such as Twenty Twenty-Five or a lightweight starter theme, and move the Elementor global styles into theme.json. That way colors, fonts and spacing are defined once, instead of in every widget.

Copy the hex values and font names from Elementor's Site Settings so the new site matches the old one. Rebuild the header and footer as template parts in the Site Editor. If you'd rather write a theme yourself, SitePoint's series on building a WordPress theme from scratch covers the basics.

Most Elementor sites repeat the same few sections: a hero, a services grid, testimonials and a call to action. Build each one once as a block pattern, then reuse it on every page. Patterns are plain block markup, so you don't need React to write them, whatever you may have heard about React and WordPress development.

In a block theme, any PHP file in the /patterns folder with a header comment is registered automatically:

For page content, there's a useful shortcut. Elementor saves a plain HTML copy of each page into post_content. Open a page in the Block Editor, and the old content usually appears in a Classic block. Choose Convert to blocks, then delete the leftovers and rebuild the layout with your patterns. It keeps the copy and images, so you only redo the design.

The difference shows up in the page source. Here's typical front-end markup for a single heading on an Elementor page, using the older section and column layout:

Newer Elementor versions with Flexbox containers and the optimized markup settings trim some of those wrappers, but most older sites still carry all of them. Multiply that by every element on every page, and the weight adds up.

To speed up this step, I built a free Elementor to Gutenberg converter. Paste in a page's elementordata JSON from Step 2, and it returns block markup you can paste into the Block Editor's code editor. It handles headings, text, images, buttons, spacers, dividers and column layouts, and lists anything it can't convert, such as forms and popups, for you to rebuild by hand. It runs entirely in your browser, so the page data never leaves your computer.

The code is rarely what holds a migration up. Content is. Once clients see their site being rebuilt, many decide it's time to rewrite their pages too, and the project ends up waiting on copywriters and approvals. My fix is to separate the two: migrate the existing content as it is, launch, then update the copy afterwards. That keeps the speed and SEO gains on schedule, and it means any ranking change can be traced to either the migration or the new copy, not both at once.

This is the step that decides whether rankings hold.

To spot-check a redirect or a status code from the command line:

When staging matches the live site, deploy it. Then:

Leave the old elementordata meta in place for a few weeks, in case you need to roll back. Once you're confident, take a backup and remove it to shrink the database.

Run PageSpeed Insights again straight away for lab scores. Field data takes longer: the Chrome UX Report uses a rolling 28-day window, so give it a month before judging real-user results. Compare Search Console clicks for the same period before and after launch.

In my migrations, the biggest change is usually page weight and loading time, because the builder's CSS, JavaScript and wrapper markup are gone. What doesn't always improve is server response time. If the site is on slow shared hosting, the first byte still arrives late, and that needs a hosting fix, not a rebuild. Search traffic should hold steady if the URLs and metadata were protected. If it drops, the Step 5 crawl comparison is the first place to look.

Moving off Elementor isn't a one-click job, but it isn't a full redesign either. Record a baseline, inventory what the builder controls, rebuild with patterns, and protect every URL. Most of the speed gain comes from removing code the site never needed. For what to tackle after that, see these practical web performance techniques. Most of the risk comes from rushing the SEO checks. Done carefully, the client gets the same site, a faster one, and an editor that comes with WordPress itself.

Currently Amazon has 392 broadband satellites in orbit and hoped to move the service out of beta by the end of this year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amazon has suffered a major setback in its plans to offer a viable Starlink rival, after 40 satellites destined for low Earth orbit (LEO) were destroyed. Just ten minutes after the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 in Cape Canaveral, Florida, on Sunday, there was an “anomaly,” meaning that the rocket “failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit.” The language seems quite vague, but the United Launch Alliance (ULA) says that data needs to be analyzed “to understand what occurred today.”

And LIFTOFF of the first Vulcan Amazon Leo mission, carrying 40 broadband satellites to low Earth orbit! https://t.co/dv1MSjJUMFNovember 15, 2026

Shortly after the triumphant takeoff clip was shared by the ULA, above, the space tech firm updated its X feed with a confirmation that its mission on behalf of Amazon failed.

Based on data received by our Launch Director, we confirm that the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 suffered an anomaly 10 minutes into the flight and failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit. Every flight generates data, and every flight teaches us something. Since the anomaly just occurred, we don’t have any specific data yet to understand what occurred today. ULA exists to provide assured access to space for our nation and for the customers who depend on us. That purpose is why we take on some of the hardest missions in spaceflight, and why we hold ourselves to the standard of mission success. Today, we did not meet that standard. We will work closely with our customer and other appropriate organizations to find the cause, determine the appropriate corrective action, and return to flight as quickly as possible. ULA is committed to full transparency with our customers, and they will have visibility into the investigation process, which utilizes rigorous methodology and corrective action. We will provide further updates when new information becomes available.November 15, 2026

Sadly for Amazon, the ULA, and folks who want a viable substitute for Starlink in the satellite internet business, this failure will surely delay the full commercialization of this service. ISPReview notes that Amazon has successfully launched 392 Kuiper communications satellites, but needs at least 500 for basic global coverage. Moreover, Amazon Leo service’s transition from the current commercial beta phase to launching properly at the end of 2026 now looks uncertain.

Reports indicate Amazon has already manufactured 1,000 satellites, but ULA, which is a joint venture of Boeing and Lockheed Martin, will need to analyze and assure customers that Sunday’s failure won’t be repeated. ULA's Vulcan vehicle had already been out of action for nine months after technical investigations into issues with its solid rocket side boosters and core BE-4 engines (made by Amazon Blue Origin).

For some context, the ULA has (or had) a great track record. Sunday's incident was its first total mission failure in its 20-year history. Early telemetry from the organization is already pointing towards some suspected causes of the destruction of the 40 satellites. Likely fault candidates include the first-stage BE-4 engines burning longer than planned, and there may have been some altitude issues near staging.

The headline failure and essential careful analysis of the anomaly won’t hold up Amazon’s other launch partners, though. A week from today, the Amazon Leo constellation is set to grow by 36 more satellites. They will be sent into orbit aboard a European Ariane 64 Block 2 (LE-04) rocket.

Amazon has approval for an initial constellation of 3,236 LEO broadband satellites, with 392 now in orbit, plus 5,105 for 5G service. Satellite tracking sites suggest Starlink already has 11,200+ satellites in orbit.

#10232. Automating Contract Signing in Node.js: Webhooks, Verification, and Storing Signed PDFs

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sending a document for signature is the easy part of an e-signature integration. One POST request and the signer gets an email. The work that actually breaks in production happens afterwards: knowing when the document was signed, trusting the notification that told you so, handling the same notification twice, and getting the final PDF into storage before the download link expires.

This tutorial builds that whole loop in Node.js with Express. An event in your app (a deal is approved, a client is onboarded, a task moves to "ready to sign") sends a contract from a template. When the signer finishes, a webhook arrives, your server verifies it, and the signed PDF plus its audit trail get saved without anyone touching them.

The code uses Firma.dev, the e-signature API I work on, because I needed a real API to make the examples runnable. The patterns are not specific to it, though. Signature verification, idempotent handlers, short-lived download URLs, and reconciliation jobs show up in almost every webhook-driven integration, and the same structure applies whether you're receiving events from a payment processor, a CI system, or another signing provider.

There's a fifth part that tutorials usually skip: a reconciliation job that catches anything the webhooks missed. We'll build that too, because webhooks are a notification mechanism, not a delivery guarantee.

Before sending anything, wrap the HTTP calls in one place. This is where authentication lives, and it's also where you handle 429 Too Many Requests once instead of in every caller. Most APIs, this one included, return a Retry-After header on a 429, so the client should honour it rather than guessing a backoff.

The 503 branch matters later. When a document has just been completed, the final PDF and its completion certificate take a moment to generate, and the download endpoint says so with a 503 and a Retry-After instead of handing you a half-built file.

The trigger depends on your product. It might be a route handler, a queue consumer, or a CRM automation calling into your service. What matters is that the function doing the sending is small and takes plain data:

Two design choices are worth calling out.

Use a single create-and-send call when you can. Many signing APIs split this into "create a draft" and "send the draft." That's useful when a human needs to review the document first, but for automation it creates a window where your process can crash between the two calls and leave an orphaned draft. A combined endpoint makes the operation atomic from your side.

Store the provider's ID on your own record immediately. Webhook payloads reference the signing request, not your deal or customer. Without that mapping you'll end up searching by email address, which falls apart as soon as one client signs two documents.

Here's the first place integrations quietly go wrong. Webhook signatures are computed over the exact bytes the sender transmitted. If Express parses the body as JSON first, you lose those bytes, and re-serialising the object won't reproduce them (key order, whitespace, and number formatting can all change). Signature checks then fail intermittently, which is the worst kind of failure.

The fix is to use express.raw() on the webhook route only, so the handler receives a Buffer:

Most webhook providers sign payloads with HMAC-SHA256 using a shared secret. The details vary, so read your provider's docs for the exact header format, but the scheme here is common: the header looks like t=1731456000,v1=5f2b..., and the signed string is the timestamp, a dot, and the raw body. Stripe uses a very similar format, and the Standard Webhooks spec describes the same idea as a vendor-neutral convention.

Each line here defends against something specific.

The OWASP guidance on cryptographic storage covers the other half of this: the secret belongs in an environment variable or secrets manager, never in the repository.

Every serious webhook system delivers at least once, not exactly once. If your endpoint is slow, returns a 5xx, or the network drops the response, the sender retries. Firma.dev, for example, makes up to three attempts per event (immediately, then after at least five minutes, then after at least an hour) and treats anything other than a 2xx as a failure, including redirects. Other providers retry more aggressively. Either way, your handler will eventually see the same event twice.

The defence is to record each event's unique ID and skip ones you've seen. In the server above that's alreadyProcessed and markProcessed. For a demo, a Set works:

In production, use your database and let a unique constraint do the work, so two concurrent deliveries can't both pass the check:

Also notice the order in the route: respond with 200, then do the work. Many providers time out deliveries after a few seconds (five, in this case). Downloading a PDF and uploading it to object storage can easily take longer than that, and a timeout counts as a failure, which triggers a retry, which triggers duplicate work. Acknowledging fast and processing asynchronously avoids that loop.

setImmediate is fine for a tutorial, but it has a real weakness: if the process dies after sending the 200 and before finishing the work, the event is acknowledged and lost. In production, push the event onto a durable queue (BullMQ, SQS, a jobs table) before responding. Step 7 covers the safety net for whatever still slips through.

Now the actual business logic. You'll usually see several event types (sent, viewed, a signer signed, declined, expired, completed). Only one of them means "the document is final":

Three things in here are easy to get wrong.

Don't treat "a signer signed" as "the document is done." With multiple signers, each one triggers its own event, and a per-signer "all complete" flag can be true on more than one of them. Key your storage logic off the single completion event.

Don't persist the download URL. Pre-signed URLs expire (this one carries an expires_at). Saving the link to your database and serving it to users later produces broken links a few minutes or hours later. Download the bytes and store them in storage you control. Swapping the fs.writeFile calls for an S3 PutObject or a Google Drive upload is a drop-in change.

Store the audit trail, not just the PDF. If a signature is ever disputed, the PDF shows what was signed, and the audit trail shows who signed it, when, and from where (timestamps, IP addresses, the sequence of views and signatures). That evidence is what makes an electronic signature defensible, which brings us to the legal side.

Retries are finite. Endpoints go down during deploys. Some providers auto-disable a webhook after enough consecutive failures, and events aren't guaranteed to arrive in order. Any one of these can leave a completed document sitting unsaved.

A small scheduled job closes that gap. Keep a list of signing requests your app considers "pending," and periodically ask the API for their real status:

Since storeSignedDocument overwrites by ID, running it twice for the same document is harmless. That's the property you want from every step in a webhook pipeline: doing it again shouldn't change the result.

None of this is legal advice, but a few facts shape how you build.

In the US, the ESIGN Act and the Uniform Electronic Transactions Act (adopted by 49 states) give electronic signatures the same legal standing as handwritten ones for most commercial documents. ESIGN also has requirements around consumer consent and record retention, which is part of why you store the final PDF yourself rather than relying on a third-party link.

In the EU, eIDAS defines three levels: simple (SES), advanced (AdES), and qualified (QES). SES and AdES cover most business contracts. QES requires a qualified trust service provider and certificate-based identity, and some documents (certain property transfers and court filings, depending on the country) require it or exclude e-signatures entirely. Check which level your document types need before you pick a provider, since not every API offers every level.

Across jurisdictions, the evidence that holds up is the same: who signed, how they were identified, when it happened, and proof the document hasn't changed since. That's the audit trail and the completion certificate, which is why Step 6 stores both.

If you're evaluating providers for a workflow like this, the questions that matter at the integration level are mostly not on the pricing page:

The send call is one line. The reliability comes from everything around it: keeping the raw body for verification, checking timestamps and comparing in constant time, acknowledging fast and deduplicating by event ID, downloading files before their links expire, and running a reconciliation job for the events that never arrive. Get those right and contract signing becomes something your app does in the background, with no one chasing signatures or copying PDFs between folders.

For the Firma.dev-specific details used here (event names, header format, rate limits), see the webhooks guide.

#10231. Fast, reliable A2UI, with swappable inference formats

We've heard your concerns about the speed of A2UI and generative UI experiences: people wait while the model generates each screen. This release gives you levers to make that faster, so you can choose the trade-off that fits each screen.

With this release, we’re happy to introduce swappable inference formats and share examples on leveraging decision models with A2UI, which help building faster and more reliable generative UI experiences. Using our new A2UI Express as an inference format you can reduce latency by up to 3x, and up-to 80x using decision models like Jev to make layout decisions. Express requires up to 3x fewer tokens to generate the same kinds of UI compared to Direct JSON. We'll dive into the details in this blog post.

A2UI is an agent-optimized protocol for generative UI, where a client advertises a catalog of trusted components as a contract, an agent sends A2UI messages describing a bespoke user interface composed from that catalog, and the client's renderer draws the UI (see Introducing A2UI).

Initially agents generated A2UI JSON transport messages directly, every curly brace and nested element. It turns out that generating JSON trees constrained by a complex JSON schema is slow and error-prone for LLMs. This coupled A2UI to LLMs, which output text, and made it difficult to integrate faster System One decision models.

Now agents can generate A2UI Express, an intermediate format optimized for inference speed and accuracy, and rely on deterministic code in A2UI's Agent SDKs to translate it into the JSON-based transport protocol. Agent builders are also free to swap in any other DSL and its associated compiler for any special use-cases, and our A2UI Agent SDK(s) make it compatible with transports and renders.

The format the model generates is what we call inference_format. You set it with one argument when you create the generator. The SDK generates the prompt instructions for that format and compiles the model's reply into standard A2UI messages. This post covers how the swap works, why we developed A2UI Express as the new default inference format, and how a decision model can build screens from layouts you define in advance.

An inference format has two parts, and the SDK generates both from your catalog's JSON schema. A prompt section teaches the model the format's syntax, and a compiler compiles the model's reply into validated A2UI messages. Because both come from the schema, a format can't describe a component or property that your catalog lacks.

You choose the format with one argument when you create the generator:

A2UI Express is available in the Python agent SDK (a2ui-agent-sdk 0.9.0 on PyPI), in the TypeScript agent SDK (@a2ui/agent on npm), and in the Dart SDK (a2ui_agent 0.0.1-wip004 on pub.dev). In TypeScript, you pass new ExpressFormatFactory() as the generator's third argument. The Python and TypeScript SDKs can both stream Direct JSON chunk by chunk, and they compile Express from the complete reply.

Express writes one statement per line, in a style close to Python. Each line assigns a component to a variable, and the variable name becomes the component ID. Arguments are positional: the compiler maps them to property names in the order your catalog schema declares them, and _ skips one. $/title binds a value to the data model, and Event("accept") declares a server action. This is the A2UI gallery's notification card in Express:

Swappable Inference formats including A2UI Express also work with macros in the Python agent SDK, which help when a screen shows data your systems already hold. The model writes a call with only an ID, such as OrderStatus("ord_42"), and your macro fetches the order and fills in the values, so every value on screen comes straight from your data. The macros README shows the setup.

Customer feedback uncovered latency and accuracy issues with direct JSON generation. To fix this, we started devising a new inference format. We experimented with four options:

We ran all four formats through the same 51-task eval on gemini-3.5-flash and compared them for thinking modes: on and off.

A2UI Express follows python like syntax and was inspired in part by OpenUI Lang, the language of the open-source OpenUI project, which also writes each component as a name = Component(...) statement with positional arguments. OpenUI Lang adds its own built-in functions and state, and the two languages aren’t yet interchangeable. The OpenUI team has proposed working toward a shared syntax in #3214. Comment if you're interested.

Based on our tests; we observed that Express inference format works just as well even with thinking off; which can give you the fastest inference times when working with full expressivity of A2UI.

Some screens have a known shape, such as a settings page, a checkout or a confirmation. For those, the model doesn't need to design a layout. It needs to choose among layouts you have already defined, and a different kind of model is built for that. For this, you would not need the full expressivity provided by A2UI Express.

Jev, from TypeSafe, is what is called a System One model, built to make fast, structured decisions that code can use directly. Jev doesn't generate text or JSON. You send it a state and a set of typed questions, and it answers each one with calibrated probabilities. A question can ask whether a statement is true (Noul), which option from a list fits (Choice), or how something scores on a rubric (Score). Jev answers many questions in one request, in roughly 100 to 300 ms.

Apparite(jev2ui), an open-source design tool, shows one way to connect a System One model to A2UI. Jev decides the structure of each screen, code builds the A2UI components from those decisions, and a small model, gemini-3.5-flash-lite, writes only the text. The questions live in a Markdown grammar written ahead of time. A blockquote asks a question, and the bullets are its options, each with criteria. This block from grammar/screen.md decides which control a settings row gets:

On a podcast app's settings screen, Jev answers Skip forward → value, Auto-download → switch and Log out → danger, each at p ≈ 1.00. Apparite builds a whole screen in three steps:

In one run of 11 prompts, the component tree was on screen in 92 to 298 ms and each screen was complete in 0.8 to 1.7 s. The text writer generated 101 to 341 tokens per screen, and both of Apparite's Jev pipelines produced valid A2UI on all 11 prompts. The project treats these numbers as a first look, since the sample is small.

Controlled generation can only produce the layouts its grammar describes. Apparite's grammar covers nine screen archetypes and twelve blocks, so screens without a known shape are a better fit for Express. A2UI Agent SDKs, don’t include a decision model inference format yet; apparite(jev2ui) project is an example on how A2UI messages could be assembled given a batch of independent decisions from a decision model.

A2UI Express is fast and keeps the full expressivity of A2UI, but a decision model making multiple independent decisions is still faster. Choose by how much of the screen you can define ahead of time:

Use Express in your agent. Install the Python SDK with pip install a2ui-agent-sdk or the TypeScript SDK with npm install @a2ui/agent, pass ExpressFormatFactory() when you create the generator, and run the model with thinking off. Your catalog and renderers stay as they are, and the Express specification documents the syntax.

To teach Gemini, or any other model that loads skills, to write Express with only your components, generate skills from your catalog with the Python SDK's SkillGenerator:

Try controlled generation. Clone the example jev2ui repository, and follow the instructions there to get started. A hosted version of Apparite(jev2ui) runs at apparite.cnfg.ai.

Earlier A2UI posts: Introducing A2UI (December 2025), A2UI v0.9 (April 2026) and A2UI + MCP Apps (June 2026).

#10225. Overlocker uses vibecoded BIOS modification to target Intel's non-K overclocking restrictions

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In an attempt to resurrect ASRock's discontinued SkyOC overclocking feature, an enthusiast has vibe-coded a motherboard BIOS using AI, potentially bringing non-K CPU overclocking to modern Intel CPUs. According to a post on X, Hardware 360º used Chat-GPT Astra to decompile and compare two versions of an ASRock BIOS, one released with SkyOC and another with the feature disabled. The AI reportedly identified the code responsible for enabling the overclocking workaround and modified it to create a BIOS ready for implementation on a compatible motherboard. Unfortunately, it remains to be seen whether the modified firmware can restore SkyOC on modern Intel platforms, as the workaround is yet to be fully tested.

For context, Intel offers multiplier-based overclocking only on its unlocked K-series processors like the Core i9-14900K and the newer Core Ultra 270K Plus. Non-K models have locked multipliers, which restrict users from pushing CPU frequencies. However, there’s another way to raise the speed of a processor by pushing the base clock itself. The base clock, or BCLK, typically runs at 100 MHz and works alongside the CPU multiplier to determine its operating frequency. For example, raising the base clock speed of a CPU to 105 MHz increases the clock speed by 5%. The only downside is that BCLK adjustments can also affect the entire motherboard, including memory and PCIe-based components, thereby potentially causing system instability.

ASRock's SkyOC technology, which was introduced for Intel's Skylake processors in 2016, allowed users to overclock locked non-K CPUs through BCLK adjustments. Certain motherboards also featured independent clock generators, allowing CPU clock adjustments without affecting the rest of the system. Intel was not particularly pleased with the workaround, and ASRock eventually had to remove SkyOC from its motherboards via BIOS updates. Additionally, Intel introduced microcode changes to prevent non-K overclocking.

The current experiment revisits that workaround by identifying the original BIOS code and adapting it for modern motherboards. Hardware 360º claims Intel's restrictions include a mechanism that triggers a shutdown when BCLK exceeds 103 MHz, and that SkyOC originally worked around this by tricking the processor with a false report of the actual BCLK value.

Finding the original code for SkyOC does not necessarily mean it can be successfully ported to a modern motherboard BIOS. Newer Intel processors use different firmware, microcode, and clock-management mechanisms, which would make adapting the workaround considerably more complicated. Flashing modified motherboard firmware also comes with its own set of risks, including the possibility of rendering the board completely unusable. Nevertheless, it is interesting to see how AI-assisted reverse engineering can help gain access to BIOS modifications that would otherwise require a considerable amount of effort.

Download the Tom's Hardware app for up-to-the-minute updates on iOS and Android. Also follow Tom's Hardware on Google News, or add us as a preferred source, to get our latest news, analysis, & reviews in your feeds.

#10223. Preventing API Contract Drift in TypeScript Applications

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A frontend application can compile successfully and still fail when it communicates with the backend.

Imagine a backend developer changes an API response from:

The backend works correctly. The frontend also builds without errors. However, a component expecting user.name now receives undefined.

This is an example of API contract drift: the frontend and backend no longer agree on the structure or behavior of an API.

The problem becomes more difficult when several developers or teams work on different parts of an application. A change made by one team may affect another team's assumptions without producing an obvious error during development.

TypeScript helps catch many programming mistakes, but its types do not automatically validate data received over HTTP. A type declaration can describe what the application expects without proving that the server actually returns it.

In this tutorial, we'll look at a practical way to make API contracts more reliable.

Consider a typical frontend API function:

The function promises to return a User, so TypeScript allows the application to access properties such as name and email.

The return value of response.json() is not automatically checked against the User interface. The interface only provides compile-time information; it does not validate the actual JSON received from the server.

the frontend may still treat the response as a User even though the data has a different structure.

This is particularly important when working with:

Independently deployed frontend and backend applications.

APIs maintained by different engineering teams.

The solution is to validate data at the boundary where it enters the application.

Zod is a TypeScript-first schema validation library. It allows developers to describe expected data structures and validate actual values against those definitions.

Now create a schema for the user response:

First, UserSchema describes the structure that the application expects at runtime.

Second, z.infer generates the corresponding TypeScript type from the schema.

This means developers do not need to maintain a separate interface and validation definition for the same data structure.

The response is initially treated as unknown, which prevents the application from assuming that untrusted data already has the expected structure.

UserSchema.parse() validates the actual response.

If the backend returns an unexpected property type or misses a required field, Zod throws a validation error instead of allowing invalid data to move silently through the application.

You can also use safeParse() when you prefer to handle validation errors without exceptions:

This approach is useful when API responses are external or when a frontend must provide more controlled error handling.

One common mistake is placing API validation logic directly inside React components.

For a small application, this may seem convenient. As the project grows, however, it becomes difficult to reuse validation rules and identify where data transformations happen.

A better approach is to separate API schemas, service functions, and UI components.

The responsibilities are straightforward:

schemas/user.ts defines the expected user data.

api/client.ts handles common HTTP configuration.

api/users.ts contains user-related API requests.

hooks/useUser.ts manages data fetching for React.

components/UserProfile.tsx displays the validated data.

This structure makes it easier to change an API implementation without rewriting the UI.

It also makes testing more manageable because validation logic can be tested independently of React rendering.

For more ideas on organizing application code, see SitePoint's guide to modern JavaScript modules.

Runtime validation protects an application when data arrives. However, teams also need a shared description of what an API is supposed to accept and return.

OpenAPI provides a standard format for describing HTTP APIs, including:

For example, an endpoint can be described using an OpenAPI document:

This document describes what the endpoint should return.

Both frontend and backend developers can refer to the same contract instead of relying on separate assumptions or outdated documentation.

Swagger Editor helps developers write and validate OpenAPI documents. It also provides a convenient way to inspect API definitions.

Swagger UI turns an OpenAPI document into interactive API documentation, allowing developers to explore endpoints and test requests.

Redoc provides another way to generate readable API documentation from an OpenAPI specification.

These tools are particularly useful when APIs are maintained by multiple developers or consumed by several applications.

Maintaining API types manually across frontend and backend repositories creates opportunities for inconsistencies.

For example, a backend developer might add a new optional property while the frontend continues using an older interface.

One way to reduce this duplication is to generate TypeScript definitions and API clients from an OpenAPI specification.

Orval is a useful tool for this workflow.

It can generate TypeScript clients and types from an OpenAPI document, helping frontend developers work with consistent endpoint definitions.

A typical configuration might look like this:

The exact configuration depends on the Orval version and the structure of the OpenAPI document.

Once configured, the client can be regenerated when the API specification changes.

This reduces the amount of repetitive code developers need to maintain manually.

However, generated types should not be confused with runtime validation. They improve consistency during development, but an application may still need to validate responses received from external systems or services that cannot be fully trusted.

Frontend developers often need to build or test features before the corresponding backend endpoint is ready.

Waiting for a complete backend implementation can slow down development, particularly when multiple teams work independently.

Mock Service Worker (MSW) helps solve this problem by intercepting network requests and returning controlled responses.

You can define a mock handler for the user endpoint:

The frontend can now make normal HTTP requests while MSW supplies predictable responses during development or testing.

You can also simulate an invalid response:

This makes it possible to check whether the application's validation and error-handling logic behave as expected.

MSW is particularly valuable because it allows frontend tests to run without requiring a live backend environment.

Runtime validation and generated clients improve reliability, but they do not automatically guarantee that independently developed services remain compatible.

For systems with multiple services, contract testing can provide another layer of protection.

Pact is a contract-testing tool that allows consumers and providers to verify their expectations about API interactions.

Consider a frontend application that expects the user endpoint to return:

The consumer defines the expected interaction.

The backend provider can then run verification against that contract to check whether its actual response satisfies the consumer's expectations.

This helps identify breaking changes before they reach production.

These tools solve different problems. A project does not necessarily need all of them, but larger applications can benefit from combining the approaches that match their architecture.

A contract is only useful if developers consistently check it when making changes.

A practical approach is to run validation and tests automatically whenever someone opens a pull request.

For example, a GitHub Actions workflow might look like this:

The api:validate script should be configured to validate the project's OpenAPI document or run the relevant contract checks.

The test command should execute the application's automated tests.

For a production project, you may also want to include:

The objective is to detect incompatible changes before they are merged.

A failed pipeline gives developers an opportunity to investigate the change while the context is still fresh.

API contracts become harder to maintain when several teams contribute to the same system.

For example, a company might have separate teams responsible for:

If nobody owns an API contract, small changes can create unexpected problems for other teams.

Assigning clear ownership helps establish who reviews changes, maintains documentation, and coordinates breaking updates.

This becomes particularly important when an organization expands its engineering capacity across locations or adds several specialized teams. Companies planning that kind of growth may also need to consider enterprise IT recruitment as part of their wider engineering workforce strategy.

Regardless of how a team is assembled, its development process should establish clear responsibilities for API design, versioning, testing, and release coordination.

A few practical conventions can make a significant difference:

Assign an owner to every important API or service.

Document breaking changes before implementation.

Require contract-related tests in pull requests.

Keep generated clients synchronized with the API specification.

Communicate deprecations before removing existing fields.

Monitor production errors after deploying API changes.

These practices help prevent a growing engineering organization from becoming a collection of disconnected services.

API contract drift is not simply a TypeScript problem. It is a coordination problem that becomes visible when applications and services develop different expectations about the same data.

TypeScript provides useful compile-time checks, but it cannot guarantee that an HTTP response matches a declared interface.

A more reliable approach combines several techniques:

Use OpenAPI to document API expectations.

Generate TypeScript clients with tools such as Orval.

Use MSW to test frontend behavior without a live backend.

Use Pact when consumer-provider contract verification is appropriate.

Run validation and tests automatically in CI/CD.

You do not need to introduce every tool at once. Start by validating external API responses, document important endpoints, and gradually automate contract checks as the application grows.

The result is a codebase where developers can make changes with greater confidence and where frontend and backend teams have a shared understanding of how their systems communicate.
