#10221. Anthropic says Claude Mythos Preview cut AMD MI355X onboarding from up to 12 months to around three days

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

If AI can write the low-level code that ties models to Nvidia, CUDA matters less.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic told IPO investors that its Claude Mythos Preview model did the software work required to get its AI running on AMD’s GPUs in about three days, with “basically no human intervention,” a job it says used to take six to 12 months. The three days cover prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs “at a useful level of performance,” with Mythos porting or writing “almost 100% of the kernels.” That kind of low-level code, which ties an AI model to one company’s chips, has long been part of Nvidia’s CUDA moat, and an AI that can write it in days would suggest that moat is eroding.

Tom Brown, Anthropic’s co-founder and chief compute officer, made the time comparison, noting to investors that the original meant using a team of “dozens of engineers.” The company already runs its models on multiple kinds of chips — on GPUs from Nvidia and AMD, Google’s TPUs, and AWS Trainium — and its fleet is managed as “one fungible pool of compute.” Brown called the early multi-chip decision “an unconventional bet.” It may have been a wise one, given that Google’s TPUs can now natively run AI models built in PyTorch, and Meta uses them in production.

Anthropic's claim that the job used to take six to 12 months comes from past work, from its “historical experience bringing up new accelerators using traditional engineering approaches,” where tailoring the code to each hardware platform was a challenge. Removing that onboarding barrier could reduce things to a purchase decision, and Anthropic started working with AMD GPUs for the first time this year, with an agreement for up to 2 gigawatts now in place.

CUDA, Nvidia’s programming platform, is very mature at nearly two decades old. AMD has an equivalent, ROCm, with HIP as its programming layer. Code written for CUDA has to be transformed to work there, and AMD even built a tool called HIPify for the process. There is still a conversion cost to switching, and AMD’s vice president of AI software, Anush Elangovan, told EE Times in April that “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in.”

AMD has been making the argument that AI can write this type of code since August of last year, presenting a research agent that fell short of human experts at the time. In September of that year, AMD CEO Lisa Su said on the Possible podcast that much of AI software is written in CUDA, which needs to be “translated to AMD,” and “a ton of that can now be done in software.”

Moving forward to July earlier this year, AMD’s senior vice president of AI, Vamsi Boppana, said its engineers were using AI models to generate GPU kernels, and Su said that when she asked whether her team was helping the Anthropic engineer working on the MI355X, she was told they didn’t need any help. Boppana said that in some cases, the resulting kernels were even “better than the most manually tuned versions.” Under its July agreement with Anthropic, AMD is also using Claude to optimize workloads for its Instinct GPUs and to speed up ROCm development.

At AMD’s Advancing AI event in July, OpenAI’s Philippe Tillet said that once OpenAI got its Helios racks, a single engineer confirmed everything worked within a few days, and that “agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers.” Before the event, AMD’s Andrew Dieckman, corporate vice president and general manager of the company’s data center GPU business, told reporters that he has had practically zero conversations with AMD’s customers about CUDA.

Published GEAK, an AI agent that writes GPU kernels; against 30 expert-written kernels it got 19 right, at 0.92 times the experts’ speed on average

Asked about AI coding tools, said much of the code written for CUDA can now be translated to AMD in software: “We’re using software to significantly optimize our customers’ code so that they can run on AMD”

Anush Elangovan, AMD vice president of AI software

Compared Claude with HIPify, AMD’s tool for converting CUDA code: “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in”

“We use a ton of AI to create the kernels that we have”; “nobody knows our architecture better than we do”

Andrew Dieckman, AMD corporate vice president and general manager, data center GPU business

“I have almost zero conversations with our customers about CUDA at this point in time”

Vamsi Boppana, AMD senior vice president of AI

AI-assisted GPU programming will “remove any remaining barriers to broad adoption”

“agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers”

Tom Brown, Anthropic co-founder and chief compute officer

One engineer asked Claude to bring up an MI355X rack and left it running “over the weekend”

Tom Brown, Anthropic co-founder and chief compute officer

Mythos “got Claude running on AMD in just three days with basically no human intervention”; the slide footnote limits the three days to prompt processing for one model

Until this week, though, all of this came from AMD or its stage, not from Anthropic to its own investors. Anthropic buys the chips rather than selling them, and it’s heading into its IPO next week, so its multi-chip approach — now with at least some numbers to back it up — carries more weight than a chipmaker’s claims. Its prospectus says it in writing, too, though without a number: “We can onboard new chip platforms in a fraction of the time it once took.”

Not stated; left running “over the weekend”

Its “leading model” on an MI355X rack, with performance climbing

Mythos, with “basically no human intervention”

Getting “Claude running on AMD”; Claude “ported or wrote almost 100% of the kernels”

Work that “took dozens of engineers months”

IPO roadshow video, slide footnote, Nov. 10

Bringing up “prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs at a useful level of performance”

Six to 12 months (“~6-12-month estimate”), from “historical experience bringing up new accelerators”

Its engineering teams, “leveraging Claude”

Bringing “new chip platforms into production”; no chip named

What it calls “intense engineering effort over many months”

The prompt processing (prefill) part of the workload is the step where a model reads and processes what it’s been given before it starts replying, and Anthropic hasn’t said whether the replying step (decode) was included as well. Anthropic’s chart, with its axis labeled “Time to onboard new silicon,” shows both lines reaching the same “~100%” mark without saying 100% of what. The company also hasn’t said how fast the model ran on AMD’s GPUs versus Nvidia’s, and that’s the comparison we want to see to show whether AMD’s chips, running AI-written code, can match Nvidia silicon.

On the other hand, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an April interview with Dwarkesh Patel that Nvidia already uses AI on its own kernels and knows its chips best — “nobody knows our architecture better than we do” — and can get the most out of them. Asked why labs like Anthropic use other accelerators, he called Anthropic “a unique instance, not a trend.” Nvidia and AMD, among seven companies, are buying $26.0 billion of Anthropic’s stock at the IPO price, so Nvidia is investing in the very company Huang called the exception.

In late July, SemiAnalysis published an article, “Can AMD break the CUDA Moat?,” which essentially said that software creation is not the same moat it was even a year ago. That said, the catch for AMD is that with agentic coding, “each agent requires GPUs to test their code against,” which means that the AI writing the code still needs the hardware. It has found a constant lack of GPUs inside AMD for this type of development, which has held AMD back and may be a new bottleneck.

That leaves CUDA’s moat somewhere in between. Getting a model running on a new chip, once the expensive part, appears to be getting cheaper, and better AI may wear down Nvidia’s claim that it knows its hardware best. As for Anthropic’s fungible pool, the chips in it aren’t fully interchangeable — Google’s TPUs are increasingly specialized, for example — and getting the most out of any of them still takes the hardware to test on. The moat is narrowing, but is not gone.

Anthropic’s first AMD MI450 chips, in Helios racks, are expected in the first half of 2027. That’s a new generation and an opportunity to see if the three-day bring-up can be repeated. Anthropic’s IPO comes sooner, after which it will report as a public company.

#10217. 10 Best AI App Builders I've Used in 2026 (and What Each One Gets Wrong)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI App Builders are exploding in popularity, and for good reason. It’s never been easier to build real, functional apps and internal tools using app builders. To test them all, I built the same app on 10 of the most popular AI app builders and tested each across usability, cost, correctness, and ease of use. Most of them can turn a paragraph of description into something that looks like software within a single session. The differences show up around the tenth prompt, when you learn what each edit costs and who has to fix the code once something breaks. I took every price from the vendor's own pricing page on October 29, 2026.

TLDR: Of the best AI app builders I've used, Softr is the one I'd hand to a team that needs auth, data, user groups or integrations. The other nine I tested include code generators such as Lovable and Bolt and visual platforms such as Bubble and Glide, and each one carries a cost or upkeep trade-off worth seeing before you commit.

I looked at the same seven things in every tool, from the first prompt onward:

Softr gets used a lot by operations and business teams as their AI app builder for secure client portals, internal tools and other business apps, none of which require code. Write a short brief in your own words, and the AI Co-Builder turns it into a database plus the core app that sits on it: tables, pages and layouts, the data connections behind them, plus user groups and permissions, so real users can sign in from day one. Workflows come as the next step, either by prompting the Co-Builder again or by adding them in the visual editor.

The Free plan is $0 for unlimited apps, with room for 5 app users, 3 builders, 5,000 records and 5 AI credits each month. Basic runs $19 per month, Pro $99 and Business $329, all on annual billing. Enterprise pricing is custom and includes SSO and SCIM for organizations managing access at scale. The full plan table sits on Softr's pricing page.

Lovable builds full-stack web apps through conversation, writing React code while it takes care of hosting, SSL and the backend. It's designed to move a founder or product team from a prompt to a shareable prototype, and the preview updates as the agent works.

Base44 calls itself a no-code AI platform: you explain the app, and it generates the structure, design and logic while running the backend itself. Authentication, a database and analytics come on every plan, Free included.

Base44 is free for 25 message credits a month. Starter costs $16 per month and Builder $40 per month, both on annual billing, with Pro at $80 and Elite at $160.

Bolt is a coding agent that writes full-stack projects in the browser and runs them on Bolt Cloud, which supplies hosting, databases and integrations. It suits people who want to watch the code take shape and edit it themselves.

Bolt Free is $0 with 1M tokens a month and Bolt branding. Pro costs $25 per month with 10M tokens, and Teams runs $30 a month for each member on monthly billing.

Replit pairs its Agent with a cloud workspace, so a description turns into a running app whose sign-in, database, hosting and monitoring come wired up. The homepage pitches it to people who don't code, though the result underneath is a code project.

Core costs $20 per month ($18 with annual billing) and Pro costs $100 per month ($90 with annual billing). Enterprise is custom and adds SSO, SCIM and audit logs.

Glide starts internal business apps from whatever you hand it, whether a spreadsheet, a written prompt or a PDF, and its newer platform, GlideOS, reads a sheet's structure and relationships to draft screens and workflows. Its apps grew up on phones, and that heritage still shapes the product.

Glide Free is $0 with limited credits. Basic costs $25 per month, Plus $50 per month and Pro $125 per month; annual billing isn't offered yet, according to Glide.

Bubble Free gives you 50K workload units and 500 AI credits each month. Web plans run $32 per month for Starter, $119 for Growth and $349 for Team, all on annual billing.

Retool is an internal tools engine aimed at engineering teams. You describe or assemble an app that connects straight to production databases, APIs and LLMs, with auth and access controls in place at deployment.

v0 generates full-stack Next.js web apps from a prompt and deploys them to Vercel, with Design Mode for visual tweaks and GitHub sync for the code. It's designed around a developer's workflow.

v0's Free plan gives new users $5 of one-time credits and caps messages at 7 per day. Plus runs $30 a month per user, and Business $100 a month per user.

Figma Make turns prompts and Figma design context into code-backed prototypes and web apps you can publish at a URL. It can also scaffold a Payload backend with a database, APIs, auth and an admin panel, or connect to Supabase for sign-in and stored data.

Figma's Starter plan is free with up to 500 AI credits a month. Figma lists a Professional Full seat at $16 per month with 3,000 AI credits, and Organization Full seats cost $55 per month on annual billing.

Collins Dictionary named "vibe coding" its Word of the Year for 2025, and the phrase now covers any workflow where you describe software in plain language and an AI produces it. AI app builders package that workflow with hosting, data and sign-in, so a non-developer can get from idea to something clickable without installing anything.

The questions buyers ask have turned plainer since then: what each edit costs, who keeps the app running, and whether a client can sign in and see their own records alone. A generated front end makes a good demo, and a team using it every day needs permissions and a predictable bill for the next hundred edits.

Softr is now my default when recommending AI app builders. It handles teams, auth, and real company data better than any other product. The AI Co-Builder drafts tables, pages and user groups, workflows follow as the next step, and the app keeps running on maintained platform components with no codebase to patch. App-user pricing and an editor that works without AI credits keep later changes from turning into a running meter.

Three situations call for a different tool. Glide makes a better mobile-first choice when the app spends its days on phones in the field. Bubble gives more front-end design freedom when a project calls for bespoke layouts, interactive animation or heavy brand styling. If your engineers want version control and audit logs and can handle a technical setup, Retool covers that need.

Agents are moving inside the apps these tools produce. Gartner expects task-specific AI agents inside 40% of enterprise applications by the close of 2026, against under 5% in 2025, so a 2027 app builder will need to host agents that respect the same permissions as human users.

Building is also leaving the builder's own window. The Model Context Protocol now sits under the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation and reports more than 97 million SDK downloads a month, with client support in ChatGPT and Claude, among others. Softr's changelog dates its official Claude and ChatGPT connector to October 6, 2026, and I expect more teams to start an app from the chat assistant they already use.

The caution comes from Gartner too: it predicts organizations will scrap upward of 40% of agentic AI projects before 2028, citing escalating costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Going into 2027, I'd judge any builder on whether it keeps agent costs and access rules visible to the team running the app.

Neville Naidoo is a technology journalist covering AI, startups, and the digital economy, with a focus on how emerging technologies are constantly evolving and shaping our everyday life.

Minisforum’s smallest and cheapest mini PC packing Intel's Wildcat Lake silicon is here. It is small, quiet, and easy to stow away, but this machine's performance is really only for light workloads.

Capable enough for home office and media consumption

Only old or lightweight games and emulators are playable

Not a cheap option unless you have spare parts and go barebones

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Minisforum’s Intel-based M2 line includes mini PCs with powerful processors such as the Core Ultra X7 Processor 358H (with 16C/16T CPU and B390 graphics), and the Core Ultra 7 356H (16C/16T). But there are more modest systems, too. The M2 Air-304 here is the most affordable option, based around the Intel Core 3 processor 304 with its 5C/5T CPU (1x Cougar Cove P-core and 4x Darkmont LP E-cores) and an iGPU with just a single Xe3 core.

No surprise, the little brother in the pack, both in terms of physical size and computing resources, is the most affordable. It currently starts at $263 for a barebones version. However, our test sample with 16GB DDR5-5600, a 500GB SSD, and Windows 11 Pro is listed at $559 - direct. That’s an introductory price, and Minisforum’s small text threatens that our configuration will bump up to $699 at some point in the future. Such a rise would put this product offering on very shaky ground, and we see B&H Photo has already raised the price at $729.

Overall, this Mini PC was pleasant to use, though. It ran quietly and only strained in gaming and productivity benchmarks which put it under loads that casual users aren’t going to be running on such a machine.

The Mini M2 Air looks stylish enough, and feels solid. Looks can be deceiving, though, and the top shell is a silver colored plastic, not a metallic alloy. Sometimes a metal shell can be used to help conduct away heat, but that seems less important with this actively cooled machine with a 15W processor. We note that there are thermal pads connecting the installed RAM and SSD to the bottom of this mini PC, which is metal.

A VESA mounting bracket is provided in the box if you prefer to hide the tiny device out of sight. It doesn’t have a remote power switch like the MSI Cubi NUC AI+ 3MG we reviewed earlier in the year, though, so you’ll have to consider reachability. A simple LED-lit power button is on the front of the device, alongside a couple of handy ports (more on the ports later). It also has a reset / clear CMOS hole on the front, and a Kensington lock slot on the rear.

Minisforum’s component choices mean this is a particularly small and light mini PC. The official M2 Mini specs state it is 112 x 112 x 37.15mm, which is roughly 4.4 x 4.4 x 1.5-inches. That’s a volume of 0.47 liters. It weighs in at just 440 grams, which is 0.97 pounds.

The power supply isn’t built-in. There’s a surprisingly bulky 65W power brick in the box, which connects to using a detachable figure-eight cable. However, this mini PC can be powered by USB-C, too, so you could pack a far more compact multipurpose power brick if portability is important.

Intel Core 3 304 (5C/5T) ‘Wildcat Lake,’ up to 4.3 GHz, 15W

Intel integrated graphics, 1x Xe3 core runs at up to 2.3 GHz, supports up to 3x displays

16GB in single SODIMM slot which can fit up to a 64GB DDR5-6400 module

Windows 11; barebone model available without OS

$559.00 limited-time price(16GB RAM, 500GB SSD, Windows 11 Pro)

On the front of the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 there are 2x USB-A 3.2 Gen 2 ports and a 3.5mm audio jack. Turn it to the side and you’ll see the microSD card slot. Then, around the back you have access to 1x USB4 (40Gbps, DP Alt Mode, PD), 1x USB-C 10Gbps, 1x USB 2.0, 2x HDMI 2.0, 2x RJ-45 2.5GbE LAN, and a DC-IN barrel jack.

Some might like to have at least one of the Type-C ports on the front, instead of two Type-As. But for its size the Mini M2 Air packs quite a few ports, including the very capable USB4, two HDMI ports, and two Ethernet ports. Realistically, a machine like this won’t be connected to oodles of wired peripherals, but if you found the port count lacking, USB4 allows for a capable and fast dock.

Wireless connectivity seems adequate, with an M.2 2230 card inside providing Wi-Fi 6E and Bluetooth 5.4. I had no problems with either during the test period. The wireless card is socketed. It's located under the SSD, making it easy to repair or upgrade down the line.

Accessing the components inside the Minisforum Mini M2 Air is straightforward, but you need to peel off all the four rubber feet to get to four long fastening screws. When they’re removed, just flip off the metal bottom to access the M.2 slots and SODIMM. Be aware there’s some adhesive force holding this metal plate in place, due to the sticky thermal pads on the RAM and storage.

There’s just a single M.2 2280 storage slot and one SODIMM slot, both populated. That means, there’s no way to upgrade the RAM than swapping the module you already have in place. Our sample came with a 16GB DDR5-5600 SODIMM, but the system supports up to DDR5-6400. This only non-barebones configuration also came with a 500GB SSD in the M.2 slot.

We have Intel’s latest 18A silicon inside this tiny PC, but not that much of it. Our review unit came with an Intel Core 3 304 "Wildcat Lake" chip that runs up to 4.3 GHz. Previous mini PCs based on Intel 300 series mobile silicon like the previously MSI and the GMKtec Evo-T2 came with a 16C/16T CPU. However, here we have just 5C/5T, and that’s just one performance core supported by four low power efficiency (LPE) cores.

Still, single core performance continues to be important for system and app responsiveness. The proof of the pudding is in the testing, though. And while this system felt perfectly usable in Windows 11, internet browsers, and word processing — preparing and writing this review, for example — the benchmarks are somewhat tougher.

As we expected, the single core tests show Wildcat Lake has some bite. It easily beats its AMD-based rivals in the POV Ray single core rendering tests, for example. Moreover, in tests like Cinebench 2024 single core, the Mini M2 Air might trail the pack, but not by very much. It's roughly 107 points is actually within touching distance of the 120 points scored by ‘high performance’ mini PCs like the Beelink SER10 MAX (Gorgon Point) and MSI Cubi NUC AI+ 3MG (Panther Lake).

Multicore tests exposed obvious weaknesses of the 5C/5T CPU configuration with single-channel RAM. All the other mini PCs we’ve tested this year come with 12C/24T (AMD) or 16C/16T (Intel), so the productivity benchmarks we run which can take advantage of multithreading for demanding rendering or encoding tasks make the Minisforum here seem weak. However, it might still be acceptable for transcoding a short video or two, occasionally, as long as you aren’t in a rush. If you are coming from an older, less efficient desktop sporting something like an i7-7700K or i5-10400, this 15W Wildcat Lake chip is an improvement in both single and multicore performance.

During our demanding tests, the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 stayed cool and quiet thanks to its 15W processor and capable cooling.

For anything other than the most casual gaming or emulation of older consoles you should look at other devices. We’ve looked at Intel 300 silicon powered devices with iGPUs packing 12 Xe3 cores, and four Xe3 cores, but this just has one, and it is further hampered by sharing the single-channel system RAM.

Prepare to see some low frame rates / scores in our standard tests. I felt that some of these tests probably shouldn’t be run on this mini PC, but did it anyway, for completeness. However, some 3DMark benchmarks simply wouldn’t run, as Intel has pruned the features that work on this iGPU due to limited resources. For example, 3DMark Solar Bay doesn’t run on this system, and neither does Speed Way. Error messages about unsupported hardware pop up when you try these tests.

Steel Nomad ran, though, and the Minisforum and its solo Xe3 core achieved a score of 135. The aforementioned Evo-T2 with Intel Core Ultra X7 358H and (B390) iGPU with 12x Xe3 cores scored 1,624. That’s incredibly close to 12x faster than the Core 3 304’s ‘Intel Graphics.’ A score of 135 means Steel Nomad ran at just a little more than 1 FPS, which actually doesn’t hurt your eyes to watch as much as 10 FPS.

Using the multi-scene benchmarks built into Shadow of the Tomb Raider and Cyberpunk 2077 using our standard settings selections resulted in some unpleasant experiences. Performance of no better than 10 or 12 FPS was observed in these tests. To achieve 25 or even 30 FPS in these titles I found that reducing the game resolution to 720p and low/lowest settings would just about get there.

So, for some actual fun on the Mini M2 Air I decided to try the latest version of the Sony PSP emulator PPSSPP and play some Wipeout Pure. I managed to run this game on my 1440p monitor with a 6x render resolution, and it achieved a nice steady 60 FPS, delivering an enjoyable futuristic racing experience. It would also therefore likely be good for retro emulation of similar era and older consoles as well as home computers.

During my day-to-day use, the Minisforum system consumed around 9W when idling on the desktop. When under the heaviest loads I threw at it, the highest system power consumption I saw was around 31W.

I didn't find it surprising that this low-power system ran cool and quiet. System tools showed that during light activities like browsing the web the CPU temperature remained in the low 30s Celsius. Playing games or benchmarking for length periods, I never saw the reported CPU temperature rise beyond the low 70s Celsius.

Minisforum’s tiny mini PC was a quiet operator at idle and under load. My measurements from about half a foot in front of the device show noise levels under 30 dBA at idle, which is very quiet and actually said to be beneath the measurement threshold of my meter. Stressing the system with simultaneous CPU and GPU loads I managed to observe just a reading of a little over 37 dBA from the system.

The Mini M2 Air ships with Windows 11 Pro installed in the configuration we received. I found no pre-installed bloatware. The official specs don’t mention compatibility with other OSes like Linux.

Minisforum sells this mini PC with a two-year warranty and a 30-day money-back guarantee.

Minisforum sells only two Elite Mini M2 Air-304 configurations in the U.S. There’s a barebones model, or the configuration we tested with pre-installed RAM, SSD, and OS.

At the time of writing our 16GB / 500GB / Windows 11 Pro configuration is listed by the mini PC maker at $559 but the product page says the price is normally $699. The barebone model is currently $263, discounted from $329. We don’t know when the prices might get adjusted. However, we’ve noted U.S. retailers like B&H Photo are now selling the 16GB / 500GB model at $729.

Though the Mini M2 Air’s performance may lack in performance, it also probably the smallest cheapest, and least power-hungry mini PC we’ve tested all year. If these kinds of qualities are important to you, the Minisforum might be appealing.

It is admirable that this mini PC is so small but leaves RAM and storage upgrades on the table for later. Other positives are the sturdy build, despite the use of plastic for the top shell, and a good port selection considering its size. In addition to being easy to hide due to its small stature, and supplied VESA mount, you probably won’t hear it very much, either.

Of course, the performance is a weakness that can’t be ignored, especially in the context of our ever-growing tested mini PCs chart from 2026. It is great to see this latest-gen processor barebones at just over $260 direct from Minisforum. If you have a spare DDR5 SODIMM and SSD, perhaps from a laptop upgrade or wherever, this might help you get a new mini PC system up and running cheaply. However, the 16GB RAM and 500GB SSD (and Windows 11 Pro license) currently more than doubles the list price (it adds $296). That’s a bit painful.

Furthermore, if the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air ever rises to its apparent MSRP of $699, we’d definitely look elsewhere. Around this price, especially during sale periods, you can shop for mini PCs employing far more powerful previous-gen processors. One good alternative candidate might be the Geekom IT13 Max 2026 we reviewed in August at $799. Price trackers show this Core Ultra 185H machine with 24GB RAM has been available for under $600 with discount / coupon over the last few months. There are also a lot of capable older gen AMD mini PCs to ponder over at attractive prices.

Mark Tyson is a news editor at Tom's Hardware. He enjoys covering the full breadth of PC tech; from business and semiconductor design to products approaching the edge of reason.

#10213. Building Conversational Commerce with Multi-Agent Swarms on GCP: A Working Prototype

Imagine ordering your weekly groceries as naturally as texting a friend:"Hey, we're out of milk and eggs. Can you also add some snacks for Friday movie night?"

In our previous post, Building Conversational Commerce with Multi-Agent Swarms on GCP, we shared the architectural blueprint for conversational commerce—proposing a 3-layer decoupled design (Ingress, Memory, and Cognition) to solve messaging timeouts, state tracking, and response reliability.

While blueprints establish the architecture on paper, production readiness demands working software. In this post, we take that blueprint and bring it to life with a functional prototype called HappyDelivery.

Using LangGraph, Gemini, FastAPI, and Google Cloud, we'll walk through the code, the state machine, deterministic financial tools, and an automated verification suite that demonstrates how this multi-agent swarm operates in practice.

To build a reliable commerce swarm, we implemented three decoupled layers that handle the journey of every customer text message:

Let's examine how each layer functions in code with its own dedicated architecture.

A frequent question when designing conversational commerce is: how does a message sent by a customer on their smartphone actually land in Layer 1, and can this architecture support messaging platforms beyond WhatsApp?

End users do not communicate directly with your Google Cloud servers. Instead, customer messages traverse an operator ecosystem before landing on your webhook:

Every major consumer messaging platform operates on the exact same fundamental contract: an asynchronous HTTPS POST webhook delivery with a strict HTTP acknowledgement timeout:

While the payload schemas and cryptographic headers differ slightly between platforms, Layer 1 abstracts away all client differences:

This ensures the rest of your system—Session Store (Layer 2) and Agent Swarm (Layer 3)—operates on clean, normalized data without caring whether the shopper sent their message from an iPhone, an Android RCS chat, or a WhatsApp thread.

Messaging platforms are completely stateless: every text message arrives as an isolated webhook event. To provide a coherent shopping experience, the system must instantly reconstruct the customer's full context without having to re-read or re-parse days of chat logs on every turn.

We use Cloud Memorystore (Redis) keyed by the customer's verified phone number (session:{phone_number}) to store active state:

A common question in conversational commerce is whether state lookups will introduce latency. Here is what the baseline infrastructure supports:

Key Takeaway: In practice, the Session & Cart Layer will never be the bottleneck—it easily outpaces upstream webhook inference and downstream LLM inference.

Rather than relying on a single monolithic prompt to handle every task, we separate reasoning into a swarm of specialized agents:

Now that we have seen the high-level design of the three layers, let's look at the actual Python prototype code that brings each layer to life.

The goal of this service is simple: verify authenticity across any inbound channel, normalize the payload into a canonical schema, and get off the critical path in < 150ms.

Here is our implementation showing how multiple messaging channels (WhatsApp, RCS, Apple Messages, SMS) are handled by pluggable adapters:

By immediately returning an HTTP 200 OK and handing the payload off to Pub/Sub, we completely decouple message intake from downstream AI reasoning time across all messaging ecosystems.

This empirically proves our architectural claim: the Ingress service validates the cryptographic signature and immediately commits the event to Pub/Sub, freeing the customer chat client without waiting on agentic reasoning.

The worker pods pulling from Pub/Sub need to instantly look up who the customer is and what is in their current cart.

Each record contains the customer ID (cust_9821), saved address, dietary restrictions, and current cart items. This guarantees that when a customer texts back hours later, their cart is restored instantaneously.

Every session lookup executes in 0.318ms, proving our architectural claim that restoring customer context and persistent cart state adds virtually zero latency to the conversational turn.

With Ingress and Redis handling incoming traffic and memory, all the decision-making lives in the LangGraph multi-agent graph.

Every turn passes through a typed data structure holding the customer's profile, active cart, and the supervisor's routing decision:

Next, we define the nodes and conditional edges that allow the Supervisor to dynamically route tasks to the right specialist:

A core tenet of our blueprint is: delegate arithmetic to deterministic tools. Generative models excel at language and reasoning, while financial calculations require exact precision. If a model computes taxes or discounts in free-form text, it may occasionally introduce calculation errors.

Instead, we bind deterministic tools to our specialist nodes. For example, the Inventory and Transaction agents use a Python calculation tool rather than computing prices in-prompt:

Worker containers pull messages asynchronously from Pub/Sub and stream LangGraph node transitions in real time.

Behind the Scenes: The InventoryAgent mutates the in-memory cart (removing Sprite and adding Coke Zero), executes calculate_cart_totals, and confirms delivery details without losing context.

We validated all core behaviors—webhook HMAC validation, catalog indexing, deterministic math, and multi-turn state transitions—with automated unit and integration tests:

A critical question when evaluating multi-agent architectures is token consumption and operational inference cost. In a naive implementation where massive prompts and entire conversation histories are repeatedly re-sent on every step, LLM API bills can quickly eat into e-commerce retail margins.

Our prototype is engineered specifically to prevent token waste through strict agent specialization, deterministic tools, and externalized Redis state:

By combining LangGraph for multi-agent workflows, Gemini for natural language reasoning, and GCP for fast ingress and persistent memory, we can build shopping experiences that feel as simple as chatting with a friend while maintaining the enterprise rigor required for commerce.

#10211. The UGREEN HomeAgent is a ‘local-first’ way to make your smart home even smarter

#10210. Intel bets on Classiq acquisition to turn Quantum efforts into commercially viable business

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Intel becomes a top-to-bottom quantum computing company.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After having spent more than a decade pursuing quantum computing research, Intel is making its first real push at commercializing its efforts. First, with the pending acquisition of quantum computing company Classiq, which has an existing list of enterprise customers. Intel says it'll be the only company that can produce qubit chips and cryogenic control electronics at scale; the deal would give it a complete top-to-bottom hardware-to-software stack, and Intel will also gain the chance to become a leading vendor of quantum computing products. Intel's acquisition of Classiq is expected to complete in the first half of 2027.

The key ingredient of that strategy is the pending acquisition of Classiq, which offers a hardware-agnostic quantum software development platform that enables engineers to design, synthesize, optimize, simulate, and execute quantum algorithms using high-level programming tools and AI-assisted automation, essentially untying software design from the complexity of quantum hardware. Meanwhile, Intel also plans to manufacture quantum processors using specialized versions of its 18A process technology, offer foundry services, and eventually sell complete quantum computers. Although the company expects its quantum business to become profitable, it acknowledges that commercial success will take time.

Intel has spent more than a decade developing quantum computing technologies, including silicon spin-qubit processors, cryogenic control electronics, and software for hybrid quantum-classical computing. However, the company has largely treated these activities as research and development projects rather than an actual business. The acquisition of Classiq, which Intel expects to complete in the first half of 2027, marks a shift toward commercialization as Classiq already has many clients and established revenue streams.

"Up until now […] our quantum computing group for the last ten years was […] purely a research and development project," said Pushkar Ranade, chief technology officer at Intel, during a media briefing. "It was not really viewed as a commercial activity."

Intel's quantum computing journey kicked off in 2015 with a research collaboration with QuTech — the quantum research institute of TU Delft and TNO — to develop quantum computing technologies, including silicon spin qubits. The idea was to leverage Intel's extensive expertise in silicon transistor development and its high-volume semiconductor manufacturing infrastructure to produce quantum processors in the same fabs that make conventional CPUs, overcoming the manufacturing scalability challenges alternative quantum computing architectures faced at the time. Intel subsequently developed Horse Ridge, a cryogenic control SoC manufactured using a 22nm process technology in 2020. Three years later, the company introduced Tunnel Falls, a 12-qubit silicon spin processor manufactured on a 300mm wafer.

Intel's quantum computing effort focuses on silicon spin qubits, which use spin states of individual electrons to store and manipulate quantum information. The company chose this approach partly because silicon spin qubits can be manufactured using equipment and processes similar to those employed for conventional semiconductor devices. This compatibility enables Intel to re-use most of its existing manufacturing infrastructure and expertise rather than build dedicated semiconductor fabrication facilities for quantum processors that yet have to become commercial products.

Despite the recent cuts at Intel, the company sustained its quantum computing project. Intel has developed two specialized versions of its 18A manufacturing technology for quantum computing: 18A-Q for silicon spin-qubit processors and 18A-CC for cryogenic control and readout electronics. To define/build 18A-Q, Intel has collaborated with Hitachi, AIST, and NEDO (three major supporters of quantum computing from Japan), and there appears to be a design kit intended to support large quantum computing systems. Without a doubt, 18A-Q and 18A-CC put Intel on the map of quantum computing chips in a big way. A couple of things should be kept in mind, though.

"The underlying device that we use, underlying process that we use to make the actual qubit device is a unique process, but it fits into those design rules," Ranade explained. "We can make these chips […] in the same fab that we use to make our CPUs."

Although Intel 18AQ uses BEOL interconnect geometry and design rules derived from Intel 18A, its underlying qubit devices require a different manufacturing process. According to Ranade, the technology requires a specialized thin-film stack and several additional manufacturing tools. Nevertheless, more than 90% of the manufacturing equipment is identical to that used for conventional semiconductor production, according to Intel.

"Intel 18A-CC, meanwhile, uses 18A-based geometry but requires additional characterization for operation at cryogenic temperatures," Ranade said. "Crucially, Intel can manufacture quantum processors alongside conventional CPUs in existing semiconductor fabs, which could reduce the capital expenditure required to scale production. […] More than 90% of the process equipment is exactly the same."

For conventional x86 architectures and IA-64 (while it was active), Intel has historically offered compilers that made software run better on its hardware (and sometimes on AMD hardware too). By contrast, with its quantum computing efforts, Intel has generally focused on hardware and while it has its own Quantum SDK that includes a compiler, this is not enough in the quantum computing realm as Classiq's offering operates at a higher abstraction level. With the acquisition of Classiq, the company intends to fill that gap.

Classiq develops a hardware-agnostic quantum software platform that applies electronic design automation (EDA) principles to quantum programming, which lets developers design, synthesize, and optimize quantum algorithms without manually constructing circuits from individual quantum gates. The company's software provides high-level modeling, automated algorithm synthesis, optimization, debugging, and execution capabilities across different quantum computing architectures, which somewhat resembles Intel's approach to its x86 compilers, albeit working at a different level.

Intel already has its Quantum SDK, which includes a C++-based compiler, runtime environment, and simulator for hybrid quantum-classical applications. Classiq's platform will sit above these tools and provide an application-development environment for programmers who lack extensive expertise in quantum computing.

Intel compares the potential significance of Classiq's software layer to Microsoft's MS-DOS, which helped establish the personal computer software ecosystem; Nvidia's CUDA, which enabled developers to exploit GPUs for general-purpose accelerated computing; and EDA tools, which abstracted high-level design descriptions from the complexities of transistor-level integrated circuit implementation and essentially set the stage for the multi-billion foundry industry today.

Furthermore, unlike Intel's quantum hardware business, Classiq already has commercial customers and generates revenue. The company employs approximately 130 people and serves more than 50 enterprise customers, including BMW, Comcast, Lufthansa, Rolls-Royce, and Toshiba. It also participates in more than 10 national quantum computing programs and supports over 50 quantum processors and simulators.

As existing quantum processors remain insufficient for many practical applications, companies can develop and optimize software ahead of hardware availability. As a result, these customers primarily use Classiq products to develop quantum algorithms, train engineers, and create intellectual property in preparation for commercially useful quantum computers.

Intel's CTO described Classiq's revenue and anticipated growth as healthy, but did not disclose actual financial figures.

"They have a very healthy revenue this year and a very healthy revenue CAGR over the coming years," Ranade said. "We hope that… we want that to continue."

It is necessary to note that Intel intends to preserve Classiq's hardware-agnostic approach, which means allowing the company to continue supporting quantum processors and simulators from competing vendors. This is essential to maintain Classiq's existing customer relationships and expand its revenue opportunities, as its clients can/will be able to develop quantum applications without committing to a particular hardware architecture. Obviously, Intel can generate software revenue regardless of which quantum computing technologies ultimately become commercially successful.

"We very much want to maintain that hardware-agnostic platform that they have developed to attract more and more customers," Ranade said.

When it comes to quantum computing, Intel's ambitions extend beyond manufacturing silicon spin-qubit processors. The company intends to become a supplier of complete quantum computing systems, which would combine quantum processors, cryogenic control electronics, classical computing resources, and software. For now, Intel has most of the building blocks required to develop complete quantum computing systems, but it still has to demonstrate that it can integrate them into commercially viable quantum computing at scale.

"We want to be in the business of building quantum foundry components, but also complete quantum systems," said Pushkar Ranade, chief technology officer at Intel.

Intel is also considering opportunities to provide quantum foundry services to other hardware developers. While its internal processor development will remain focused on silicon spin qubits, the company is open to manufacturing components for alternative quantum computing technologies, which somewhat resembles its approach to its x86 CPU and foundry businesses.

"We are committed to spin qubits," Ranade said. "But we are also open to providing foundry services to other modalities."

Intel believes that its capabilities in photonics, advanced packaging, and semiconductor manufacturing could become commercially relevant even to companies developing quantum processors based on different physical principles than silicon qubits.

Intel's decision to expand its quantum computing activities with the takeover of Classiq comes as chief executive Lip-Bu Tan emphasized profitability, financial discipline, and high-margin products across the company’s established businesses. Given that Intel's quantum computing initiative barely generates any revenue, let alone profits, the CEO stance raises questions about the viability of quantum computing for Intel, especially in a situation where the industry has yet to demonstrate widespread practical applications that justify the cost of large-scale quantum hardware.

When asked how Intel’s quantum computing strategy fits Tan's profitability requirements, Ranade explained that the company's immediate financial priorities primarily concern its established client and server processor businesses. Nevertheless, Intel must continue investing in emerging technologies that could shape future computing markets.

"This is really a bet on the future," Ranade said. "This is something we also have to do in addition to our mainstream businesses today."

The Intel CTO acknowledged that quantum computing is not set to become a high-margin business in the near future. Meanwhile, the acquisition of Classiq gives Intel an established commercial software operation and revenue stream here and now, as well as an opportunity to expand customer relationships well before its own quantum processors reach commercial maturity. Intel also hopes that combining its hardware and software capabilities (probably including Classiq) will attract customers interested in complete quantum computing solutions.

When asked whether vertical integration would make Intel's quantum computing operations profitable, Ranade acknowledged that he could not predict gross margins of the quantum computing business because the industry is still in its infancy. Nevertheless, he expressed confidence in the commercial prospects of Intel's quantum hardware and software.

"I expect it to become a profitable business very quickly on the hardware side, but too early to tell," Ranade said.

Even though quantum processors evolve at a rapid pace, the technology is unlikely to replace existing CPUs and GPUs for conventional computing any time soon. Instead, Intel expects quantum computing systems to operate alongside the usual processors in data centers and supercomputing environments.

Classiq's software platform will provide high-level quantum algorithm development capabilities, while Intel Quantum SDK will handle compilation, simulation, and hybrid quantum-classical execution. This approach differs from Nvidia's CUDA-Q, which is designed to integrate quantum computing workflows with existing accelerated computing infrastructure. According to Classiq chief executive Nir Minerbi, Classiq operates at a higher level of abstraction and can complement CUDA-Q rather than compete with it.

"CUDA Q is very much low-level; it is gate-level. It is a good platform, by the way, but it is very low-level," said Minerbi. "The reason companies like Rolls-Royce and BMW and others use the Classiq platform on top of CUDA Q is due to the level of abstraction. It is much, much easier to develop quantum programs with Classiq and then to simulate it with CUDA Q rather than only with CUDA Q."

Meanwhile, commercial success of quantum computing still depends on substantial advances in quantum hardware, including qubit scalability, error correction, control electronics, and system integration. For now, many Classiq customers are developing applications for quantum computers that have yet to reach the scale required for commercially valuable workloads.

Intel expects to complete its acquisition of Classiq in the first half of 2027, subject to regulatory approvals and customary closing conditions. The company has not disclosed the purchase price or other financial terms.

The transaction will bring Classiq's software development capabilities and established customer relationships into Intel's quantum computing organization, but will preserve the platform's compatibility with competing hardware technologies. For Intel, the acquisition represents an opportunity to turn more than a decade of quantum computing research into a commercial business unit as it will have all the building blocks required to create quantum systems. Furthermore, Intel's quantum computing business will be open to third parties in the sense that Intel will be able to build quantum processors for partners using its 18AQ and 18ACC process technologies.

Whether Intel's quantum computing business eventually delivers the high margins Intel seeks will depend not only on Intel's ability to build systems, produce quantum chips for others, or Classiq's software revenue, but also on the industry's ability to develop quantum processors and systems capable of solving real-world problems that people are willing to pay for.

#10208. Top 6 AI Detector Tools in 2026: Features, Accuracy Claims and When to Trust Them

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI writing tools are now part of everyday work for students, marketers, developers and editors, and a parallel category of tools has appeared to answer one question: was this text written by a person or a model? Teachers, publishers, hiring teams and content managers use these detectors to screen submissions, but the technology is probabilistic, its accuracy claims vary, and the cost of a wrong call can be high.

This guide compares six AI detector tools, CudekAI, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Originality.ai, Turnitin and Sapling, on what their own pages state. It then explains how detectors work, summarizes the research on their limits, and ends with a short script you can use to test any detector on your own samples. The tools are listed in no ranking order. Feature details, limits and prices change often, so confirm current information on each vendor’s site before you commit.

• A detector gives a probability, not proof. Every vendor in this list warns that results can be wrong, and several advise against using a score alone.

• Limits differ widely. Character limits, supported languages, models detected and free allowances vary between tools and even between pages of the same tool.

• Bias and short text are known weak spots. Research has found detectors misclassify some non-native English writing, and most vendors say accuracy improves with longer text.

• Vendor accuracy claims are not comparable. Figures come from different datasets, thresholds and models, so test on your own material.

• Use detection as one signal. Combine it with drafts, version history, interviews or other evidence before acting on a result.

CudekAI’s AI detector returns an AI percentage and a human percentage for pasted text, with paragraph, sentence and word-level analysis for mixed documents. According to its page, it also accepts PDF and Word files with per-page scores and downloadable reports, and offers separate code and image detectors. The page lists models it says it detects, including ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Llama, DeepSeek and Grok, and says it supports more than 100 languages.

Strengths: sentence-level highlighting, file uploads with reports, and extra detectors for code and images in the same product.

Limitations and gaps: the page shows different character limits in different places, including 1,000, 5,000 and 15,000, so check the limit that applies to your plan. It says to start free but does not state a free allowance, and it lists detection as costing one credit. A headline accuracy figure of 99 percent appears in its page metadata, while the body refers to independent benchmarking without naming sources, and its FAQ says accuracy may vary for mixed or paraphrased text and that false positives can occur. API access is listed under an enterprise plan.

Fits: writers and teams who want a quick check with sentence-level detail and file reports, and who will verify results rather than rely on a single percentage.

GPTZero gives an overall score and highlights flagged sentences, and adds a Writing Replay feature that records the writing process, including typing patterns and paste events. It also lists a plagiarism checker and integrations with Google Docs, Google Classroom, Canvas, Zapier and a Chrome extension, plus an API.

Strengths: process-based evidence through Writing Replay, strong education integrations and published benchmark figures. On the RAID benchmark, the page reports 95.7 percent of AI text detected and 1 percent of human text wrongly flagged.

Limitations and gaps: it claims full support for five languages (English, German, Portuguese, French and Spanish) and says other languages lack stated accuracy levels. The company’s own page says no detector is 100 percent accurate, that results should not be the final verdict, and that accuracy is higher on longer English text. The free scan box has a 10,000-character limit, and plan prices are not listed on the page.

Fits: educators and editors who want writing-process evidence as well as a score.

Copyleaks describes an AI detector covering more than 30 languages and models including ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Llama. Its AI Logic feature explains why text was flagged, and it says it can detect AI-generated code. It offers a browser extension, a Google Docs add-on, learning-management-system integrations and an enterprise API.

Strengths: explanation of flagged text, code detection, broad integrations and published per-language accuracy figures, which range from 93.08 percent for Portuguese to 99.20 percent for English on AI text, according to the page.

Limitations and gaps: the figures are the vendor’s own. It claims a 0.03 percent false-positive rate and cites a 2023 study, so treat the number as a claim to verify on your data. Free scans cover up to 25,000 characters, but paid plan prices sit on a separate pricing page.

Fits: organizations that need multilingual coverage, code detection or integrations.

Originality.ai combines AI detection with a plagiarism checker and a fact and hallucination checker. It lists sentence-level highlights, a mixed AI and human category in its newer detection models, a Chrome extension with a writing replay feature and an AI detection API.

Strengths: several checks in one product, an API with documentation and a clear statement of limits. The page acknowledges false positives, says very short text under a 100-word minimum and AI rewriting tools can affect results, and advises against using the score alone.

Limitations and gaps: it is credit-based, free scans are capped at 100 credits per account and credits expire two years after purchase. Its accuracy claims cite third-party studies and internal benchmarks that you should review yourself.

Fits: publishers, agencies and SEO teams that want detection, plagiarism and fact checks in one workflow.

Turnitin is built for educators and institutions. Its AI writing detection estimates how much of a submission is human-written versus likely AI-generated, including likely AI-paraphrased content, and works inside learning-management systems alongside similarity checking.

Strengths: deep institutional integration and unusually explicit framing of results as probabilities. For documents above its 300-word minimum, the page reports false-positive rates of 0.014 for English language learners and 0.013 for native English writers and concludes there is no statistically significant bias. It also links guidance on handling suspected false positives.

Limitations and gaps: it is aimed at institutions rather than individual freelancers, the page does not give an overall false-positive figure, and it describes detection as just one piece of a broader challenge. Mention of language support is limited, with a linked post about Arabic.

Fits: schools and universities that already use Turnitin and want detection within the same workflow.

Sapling offers an AI content detector with a free check of the first 2,000 characters, and Pro and Enterprise accounts can check up to 100,000 characters. It provides a free API key and documentation, and it publishes unusually candid guidance on limits.

Strengths: transparent caveats and a developer-friendly API. The page says false positives and negatives may occur, that accuracy varies with dataset, text length and threshold, and advises measuring results on your own examples. It also warns that some tools claiming to be undetectable falsely say they passed its detector.

Limitations and gaps: accuracy improves after about 50 words, non-English text is scored by the English model for unsupported languages and is described as less reliable, and prices are not listed on the page. A reported 60 percent drop in false positives comes from internal evaluations that the page does not publish.

Fits: developers and teams that want to integrate detection and test it on their own data.

The table summarizes what each page states. It is not a benchmark, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

• False positives. Human writing can be flagged, especially when it is short, formulaic or highly predictable.

• Bias risk. A research paper titled GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers found that the detectors it tested consistently misclassified non-native English writing as AI-generated, and warned against using such detectors in educational settings. Turnitin’s page reports the opposite for its own tool, which is a reason to test on your population.

• Evasion. Paraphrasing or editing AI text can lower scores. The same research found that simple prompting strategies could both reduce bias and get text past detectors.

• Mixed authorship. A document that is partly AI-assisted can get a low overall score while individual sentences score high, as Sapling’s page points out.

• Moving targets. New models change the text distribution, so a tool trained on older models can lose accuracy.

• Shifting benchmarks. Vendors report different datasets and thresholds, so numbers are not comparable.

OpenAI’s own experience is a useful reminder. Its AI text classifier correctly flagged 26 percent of AI-written text on a challenge set while mislabeling 9 percent of human text, and the page states that the classifier was withdrawn on July 20, 2023 due to its low rate of accuracy. Detection has improved since, but the underlying difficulty has not gone away.

Most detectors are classifiers trained on large collections of human-written and machine-generated text. They look for statistical patterns, such as how predictable the word choices are or how uniform the sentence structure is, and output a probability. Some systems add features such as sentence-level scoring, writing-process data, or checks for hidden provenance signals.

• Statistical signals. Text that a language model finds highly predictable tends to look machine-like, which is why formulaic human writing can be misflagged.

• Trained classifiers. Detectors learn from labeled examples and update as new models appear.

• Writing-process evidence. Tools such as Writing Replay record how a document was typed or pasted, which is stronger evidence than a style score.

• Provenance. Standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to record the origin and edits of content when the creating tool supports them, which is different from guessing after the fact.

Because vendor figures are not comparable, the most reliable approach is to measure. Build a small labeled set that reflects your real content, including human writing from different groups of authors, and calculate false-positive and false-negative rates by group. The script below wraps any detector behind a single function. The stub is a placeholder, so replace it with a call to the tool you are evaluating and follow that vendor’s API documentation.

is_ai: bool        # ground truth you control

group: str = "all" # e.g. "native", "non-native", "technical"

def evaluate(detect, samples, threshold=0.5):

"""detect(text) -> probability the text is AI-generated (0..1).

Wrap any vendor API or tool export in this function."""

g = stats.setdefault(s.group, {"fp": 0, "human": 0, "fn": 0, "ai": 0})

g["fn"] += not flagged          # AI text that slipped through

g["fp"] += flagged              # human text wrongly flagged

fpr = g["fp"] / g["human"] if g["human"] else float("nan")

fnr = g["fn"] / g["ai"] if g["ai"] else float("nan")

print(f"{group:12} false positive rate {fpr:.1%}  false negative rate {fnr:.1%}")

return 0.9 if "delve" in text.lower() else 0.2

Sample("Let us delve into the topic.", True, "native"),

Sample("I wrote this note by hand.", False, "native"),

Sample("We delve into methods used here.", False, "non-native"),

Sample("The results were reviewed twice.", True, "non-native"),

With only a handful of samples the numbers mean little, so use a larger, representative set and report results per group, because an acceptable overall rate can hide a high rate for one group.

• Never treat a score as proof. Use it to start a conversation, not end one.

• Gather corroborating evidence. Drafts, version history, writing-process data and discussion with the author carry more weight than a percentage.

• Tell people how detection is used. Publish a policy so authors know what to expect and how to appeal.

• Check for bias. Test on writers like those you evaluate, including non-native speakers.

A tool that estimates how likely a piece of text is to have been generated by an AI model, usually as a percentage, often with sentence-level highlighting.

They can be useful, but accuracy depends on the text, length, language and threshold, and every vendor in this guide states that errors can occur. Test on your own samples.

Yes. False positives are acknowledged by vendors and documented in research, particularly for short or formulaic text and some non-native English writing.

Free tiers are fine for a quick check, but they have limits on length and features, and the result should not be the sole basis for a decision.

Sometimes. Paraphrasing and editing can lower scores, and partly AI-generated documents can produce mixed results.

A plagiarism checker looks for matches with existing sources. An AI detector estimates whether text resembles machine-generated writing, which is a different and less certain task.

AI detectors are useful screening tools, but none is a verdict. The six reviewed here differ in language coverage, limits, integrations, explainability and how candidly their pages describe error, and their accuracy figures come from different tests. Compare them on the factors that matter for your content, measure false positives on your own samples, and combine any score with other evidence before acting on it.

The Acer 3-in-1 Charging Station is a portable device that can simultaneously charge your iPhone, Apple Watch, and AirPods. Wireless charging supports up to 15 watts.

The Eissix MagSafe Power Bank offers 12,000 mAh of capacity for your smartphone and includes a built-in stand. This is perfect for keeping your smartphone battery topped off if you're frequently far away from a power outlet.

First appearing earlier this month, Google Pixel phones are rolling out a handy new Family feature in the Settings menu. Here’s what it can do.

Rolling out now via what appears to be a server-side update (tied to Google Play Services), Google Pixel phones are adding a “Family” section to the Settings menu. The feature is appearing for us on a Pixel 11 Pro XL running the October 2026 update (not Android 17 QPR2), while a Pixel 10 Pro has also just surfaced the new UI.

This new section, which Google has previously referred to as “Family Hub,” gives users a quick place to see their family members and settings in one central location. It shows a list of your family members – child accounts and anyone attached to your Google One family plan – with controls over Google One storage, Google Play Family Library, shared payment methods, and “Family events” in Google Calendar.

You can also see the live location of any family members you are sharing your location with or start sharing your location with another family member. You can also easily remove or add members to your family plan via this new menu. Each family member gets their own page where you can see benefits shared, location, and also if they are a “parent” in the family – with “remove” and “transfer” options available.

I've had it for a few weeks, and while it's surely something useful for those with a "family setup", it's IMHO a major annoyance for those who don't (who, I suspect, are the vast majority).

Couldn't they have it show up only for those "in a family"?

It's there, at the top, doing absolutely nothing useful for the vast majority of users, and pushing down all other options.

While this won’t be helpful for all users, it’s certainly full of useful shortcuts and tools for those at the center of a family plan and looking to share benefits with their household.

As far as we can tell, this is widely rolled out to Pixel owners at this point. As mentioned, we’re seeing it across multiple devices, and 9to5Google readers mentioned they’re also seeing it live even prior to installing the latest system update. The folks at Droid-Life also mention now seeing it on their Pixels.

#10201. How Much Latency Does a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Add? Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A self-hosted LLM gateway sits between your app and the model provider, so every call pays for one extra hop. We measured that cost for three open-source gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each one ran on the same machine, in front of the same OpenAI model. LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to every non-streaming call. AcruxCore added 15 to 24 ms. Langfuse's number could not be told apart from zero.

The bigger lesson was about the measurement itself. Before we controlled for it, one OpenAI network address moved our results by more than any gateway did.

Each number is the median time a gateway added to a direct OpenAI call, with its 95% interval in brackets. A gateway can log each call, and logging takes time, so we ran the test three times with three logging levels.

The direct call took 781 to 831 ms at the median, so even LiteLLM's cost is under 9% of a short call. Read the tables this way:

The obvious test is to time 100 calls through the gateway, time 100 direct calls, and compare the two medians. That test gives the wrong answer, for two reasons.

The first reason is noise. OpenAI's own response time moved by hundreds of milliseconds between calls in our runs. The direct call's p95 was 1,047 to 1,233 ms against a median near 800 ms. A gateway that adds 20 ms is hidden inside that spread unless the test removes it.

The second reason is the network path to OpenAI. It does not stay the same between a direct client and a gateway, and that difference can be larger than the gateway itself.

api.openai.com resolves to more than one address. From our network, DNS returned two Cloudflare addresses, and one was much farther away than the other. We measured both with a script that pins one address at a time:

On a connection that is already open, both addresses answer in the same time. On a new connection, the far address adds about 250 ms, because the TCP and TLS handshakes take that much longer.

A gateway keeps its connection to OpenAI open between calls. It pays the setup once and then stays on whichever address it reached first. A client that opens a new connection for each call pays the setup again on every call. How much it pays depends on which address DNS returns that time.

Node's built-in fetch closes an idle connection after about 4 seconds. So a test client that calls OpenAI every few seconds reconnects on almost every call. In our first, uncontrolled run, the Langfuse gateway came out 43 ms faster than calling OpenAI directly. A gateway cannot be faster than the call it forwards. The direct client paid for new connections, and the gateway did not.

The fix has two parts. First, pin api.openai.com to one address on every path. In Docker, that is an extra_hosts entry on each gateway container:

Second, let the direct client keep its connection open, as a gateway does. With undici, the HTTP client behind Node's fetch, that is a custom Agent:

We then listed each container's open connections to confirm that every path used the same address. The edge-check script is in the repository linked at the end, and it takes about a minute to run from your own network.

This advice also applies outside benchmarks. If your app calls OpenAI directly from Node, keep the connection open between calls. Otherwise some of your calls pay the handshake, and on a far address that costs about 250 ms.

Each test sent 150 rounds after 5 warm-up rounds. One round calls OpenAI directly and through each gateway, once without streaming and once with streaming. The order rotates every round, so no path always goes first or last.

For each round, the script takes the gateway's time minus the direct call's time in that same round. Calls in one round are made within a few seconds of each other, so a slow moment at OpenAI hits all four paths together and cancels out of the difference. The script then reports the median of the 150 differences.

The interval comes from a bootstrap. The script draws 150 differences at random, with replacement, and takes their median. It repeats that 2,000 times, and the middle 95% of those medians is the interval. This is a shortened version of the script's function:

An interval that includes zero means the test could not separate the gateway's cost from zero. That is what the Langfuse rows show. It does not prove that the cost is zero.

Every call used OpenAI gpt-6-luna with reasoning_effort: "none", the same short prompt and a cap of 5 output tokens. A short answer keeps the model's own time small, so more of the measured time belongs to the path. Each streamed call measured the time to its first token. Each non-streamed call measured the time to the full response.

All three gateways did real work on every call, which made the comparison fair:

The three logging levels mapped to these settings:

The machine was an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with 62 GB of RAM, running Fedora Linux and Docker 29.9. All three stacks ran on it at once, and the test was run on 10 November 2026.

LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to the full response, but only 9 to 43 ms to the first streamed token. The first token arrives as soon as the model starts to answer, so time spent before that point shows up in both numbers. The larger cost without streaming suggests that most of LiteLLM's extra time is spent after the model has answered. We did not profile LiteLLM to confirm this.

Logging level made no clear difference for any gateway. Adding LiteLLM's Langfuse callback did not raise its cost. AcruxCore's median rose from 15 ms with logging off to 24 ms with full logging, but its intervals overlap across all three runs. AcruxCore's median is 16 to 24 ms above Langfuse's, and those intervals overlap too, so this test does not rank the two of them.

If 60 ms per call matters to you, as in a chat app that makes several model calls per user message, LiteLLM's cost is real and repeatable. Langfuse and AcruxCore stayed near or within the noise. If your calls take several seconds, any of the three costs less than OpenAI's own variation from one call to the next.

Before you trust a gateway number, your own or anyone else's, check two things. Was each gateway compared with a direct call in the same round? And did the direct client keep its connection open on the same OpenAI address as the gateway? Without both, the result measures the network path and not the gateway.

The scripts, Compose files and raw results are on GitHub: scripts/comparison/langfuse-gateway-vs-litellm-vs-acruxcore.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems.

Following reports that ousted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is plotting a return to the company, President Donald Trump has voiced his own support for such a move, Bloomberg reports. However, according to new insight from CNBC, Nvidia's board of directors is not considering the move and is instead focused on its ongoing investigation into Huang's extramarital affairs and a national security investigation, likely into possible allegations of chip smuggling into China.

In the wake of reports that US officials were said to be concerned about his close relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, it later transpired that Huang had indeed had an affair with Ms. Huang, and that there were suspicions she was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Since that news broke, it has further been revealed that Huang also had an inappropriate relationship with one of his direct reports, Nvidia's VP of health care, Kimberly Powell, and that the company covered up the fling. Perhaps more concerningly for Jensen Huang, it has been alleged that Ms. Huang and Megaspeed may have been involved in chip smuggling into China to the tune of more than $7 billion, with hundreds of thousands of H100 and H200 chips said to have found their way into the country at her behest, a factor analysts believe may be the true reason behind Huang's exit.

The company's shares have tumbled sharply in the last week, perhaps understandably. Shares were down more than 4% on Monday, and more than 10% over the last five days, with the stock on course to lose nearly one-third of its value in the last six months.
