#10198. Pixel Tag is a brilliant Band-Aid on a Find Hub network Google can’t easily fix

The Google Pixel Tag is here and, at least so far, it’s really good. But fresh hardware and some new features can’t fix the core problem. Google’s Find Hub network has been restricted from the very start, and it’s not something Google can easily fix.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The good news on Pixel Tag: this is a well-built tracker that, unlike others, is the clear product of the hardware having direct access to the software. Little touches like being able to adjust ringer volume on the fly combine nicely with UWB – the latter something we rarely see on third-party devices largely due to Apple – all things that elevate the Find Hub tracker to what we called the new “gold standard.”

The bad news: this is still a Find Hub tracker.

Google’s Find Hub network has improved dramatically over the two years since its launch. Where Google originally had to admit that tracking performance was lacking (a nice way of putting it, frankly), improvements started arriving in 2025. We noticed that the network seemed to be working much better a few months after its wide rollout, and Google later confirmed speed improvements.

But, ultimately, the Find Hub network is still reeling from how it debuted.

Again, it’s gotten better since then, but it’s still not as good as it should be. Samsung’s network, limited solely to Galaxy phones, shouldn’t outperform Google’s. Find Hub trackers should be just as good as AirTags and the third-party trackers that work with Apple’s network. They’re just not, and I truly do believe it all boils down to this initial choice. One of the reasons this is getting better is that Google has been pushing users to the “all areas” setting during the setup of a new device – I’ve noticed this on some recent devices I set up, but it’s not been completely consistent just yet. That’s a good step forward but, even if it is the new default, that still requires millions of users to upgrade their devices, and we’ve never been in a better time to not upgrade your phone.

All of that is to say, the Pixel Tag isn’t fixing this problem. Surely, it’ll give Google some needed fire to fix the problem, but new hardware isn’t the solution. That’s something I was discussing with 9to5Google readers on our review. UWB is a tentpole feature of the Pixel Tag, but it doesn’t change the network problems, it just improves precision finding. We needed a first-party tracker like this regardless of the state of the network, something that really takes advantage of the platform and pushes it forward, but it’s just a quick fix, a Band-Aid.

What do you think? Have you found the Find Hub network reliable enough, or is there still room for improvement? Sound off in the comments below!

Google this week launched yet another new Gemini model, Hydrogen, which is a refresh of its Flash tier model and a “major standalone upgrade” on top of Gemini 3.8 Flash. Hydrogen is already powering AI features in Google Search.

#10196. Nvidia announces Rubin HPC GPU with 'record number of CUDA cores' at Supercomputing 2026

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Rubin HPC puts "over 50,000" CUDA cores on a chip, implies four-die layout

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Supercomputing 2026 conference is under way, and despite continuing turmoil elsewhere in the company, Nvidia has lots to reveal at the show. Foremost among its announcements today, Nvidia took the wraps off a new member of the Rubin GPU family called Rubin HPC, a chip that the company says is meant to accelerate agentic workflows and simulation for scientists, engineers, and analysts working with large data sets.

To accelerate those most demanding workloads, Nvidia says Rubin HPC has the highest number of CUDA cores on any of its GPUs - "more than 50,000" - which might imply that this chip uses a configuration similar to the four-die Rubin Ultra GPU, which links those dies together using NVLink-C2C.

Today's Rubin GPU has 224 SMs and 28,672 CUDA cores, so scaling compute resources to the degree Rubin HPC does would necessarily require more dies on a package. Nvidia didn't reveal more details of the chip's physical layout alongside the announcement. (The representative image shared by the company wouldn't appear to take this into account.)

To keep data moving between Rubin HPC GPUs, Nvidia will use its NVLink 7 interconnect. The company didn't share any new details of the bandwidth or physical layer of this interconnect within the rack, but past disclosures about the tech have indicated that it retains the 3.6 TB/s of per-GPU bandwidth that NVLink 6 delivers while enlarging the size of its scale-up domain.

Nvidia didn't share any details of the Rubin HPC GPU's HBM capacity or bandwidth, and it also didn't share whether this part includes a rebalancing of compute resources towards native FP64, as AMD and Intel are touting at the conference with their own GPUs this week.

Nvidia instead touts the suitability of this chip for scientists and engineers using agentic workflows, for which the company expects faster simulation performance and therefore faster opportunities for discovery in those fields thanks to better agent performance in those domains.

In fields like climate research, genomic analysis, and financial simulation, Nvidia says the Rubin HPC GPU's "balanced design," with both higher compute resources and (presumably) faster data movement between nodes, will help feed the chip with the massive data sets typical of those workloads, minimizing idle compute resources and achieving better utilization.

Researchers, engineers, and analysts marshaling agent swarms to explore the problem spaces in their domains will also be able to take advantage of Nvidia's CUDA-X domain-specific libraries to boost performance on Rubin HPC, tightening the reasoning loop from hypothesis to simulation to refinement and hastening the pursuit of the most promising results.

Nvidia says Rubin HPC will arrive sometime in 2028.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

#10193. Google Maps rolling out travel itineraries & list search for Ask Maps

Ahead of the holiday season, Google Maps is launching a handy travel itineraries feature alongside a slew of other new capabilities for Ask Maps.

Ask Maps can now build travel itineraries that feature numbered map pins for each stop. It supports everything from shopping excursions to multi-day road trips with several stops. When used for the latter, Google Maps will generate a day-by-day schedule with tips and the numbered pins that aid visualization. Plans can be shared with others and exported to Google Docs on desktop.

Once on the trip, Ask Maps will show directions and real-time details, including live traffic conditions, whether a place is open, and “even what the vibe is at your next stop.” You can conversationally have Google add more stops to the itinerary.

Meanwhile, Ask Maps can now tap into your saved lists to find bookmarked places and take action. This lets you ask: “Which spots saved in my lists have availability for five people in the next 30 minutes?”

Before you start directions, you’ll see familiar “Know Before You Go” tips that surface information about your route, like parking locations, car charging options, weather, potential delays, and tolls.

New agentic capabilities let Ask Maps find online beauty, pet care, auto, and home repair appointments, as well as call local businesses on your behalf. For example, “Find me a 4 p.m. nail appointment near me” returns a list of open slots.

Finally, Ask Maps is expanding to 30 more countries across Europe, including Spain, Germany, and the UK.

These updates are rolling out starting today on Google Maps for Android and iOS. Travel itineraries and list search are available in all 200+ Ask Maps countries. Know Before You Go in directions and appointment booking are coming first to the US.

#10192. Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

LiteRT in Google Play services is Android's on-device inference runtime for custom models, handling more than a trillion inferences a day across billions of devices. Today, it gets its biggest upgrade yet: GPU inference that runs about 1.4x faster than the legacy GPU delegate, powered by ML Drift and available through the new CompiledModel API, LiteRT’s modern successor to the legacy Interpreter API.

Until now, taking advantage of this faster GPU stack required bundling LiteRT in your app. In our testing, apps that switched to LiteRT in Google Play services are about 8 MB smaller, making them faster to download and lighter on users' devices, because the runtime, including the GPU accelerator, ships once with Play services rather than inside every app. More than 100K Android apps bundle their own copy of LiteRT or TensorFlow Lite today, so the savings add up quickly as apps switch.

If your Android app runs custom ML models on CPU or GPU, we encourage you to switch to LiteRT in Google Play services. You can see it in action in the Google AI Edge Gallery app, where the Scrapbook feature's image segmentation runs on LiteRT in Google Play services.

Developers that adopt LiteRT in Google Play Services enjoy immediate benefits:

LiteRT is the evolution of TensorFlow Lite. It keeps the same .tflite model format you rely on and adds the CompiledModel API, which makes acceleration across fragmented hardware reliable:

Whether you're starting fresh or switching from bundled LiteRT, using LiteRT in Google Play services takes a few lines of code:

If you're already using the legacy Interpreter API in Google Play services, your Interpreter code keeps working on CPU when you switch to play-services-litert. To run on the GPU, move to the CompiledModel API.

Let your coding agent do the work. In Agent Mode, the new LiteRT skill for Android Studio automates the move to LiteRT in Google Play services and the CompiledModel API. Ask it to “migrate my TFLite code to CompiledModel,” or type @litert-runtime, and it switches your app to play-services-litert, rewrites your Interpreter calls, and sets up GPU acceleration with CPU fallback. It can also scaffold a new LiteRT app. Treat it as a strong first pass: review the diff, build, and compare outputs on a real device. Get the skill on GitHub.

Upgrading from legacy APIs? The legacy GPU delegate and custom delegate APIs are deprecated in favor of CompiledModel GPU acceleration and the Compiler Plugin and Dispatch APIs, and NNAPI will be removed starting in 2027. Older SDKs now receive critical security fixes only. Apps you've already shipped keep working, but plan your move to the CompiledModel API now: code that uses deprecated APIs won't compile against the latest SDK, and staying on an older SDK can cause conflicts as your other dependencies move forward. See the release notes for details.

Looking ahead, LiteRT in Google Play services is our foundation for running your own task-specific models on Android, with CPU and GPU acceleration.

#10191. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

#10190. AMD grabs over 40% of new TOP500 list for supercomputers, up 14% since last year

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Lux is the first of two machines AMD is developing as part of a $1 billion deal with the DOE.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With the coming of SC26, also comes a fresh edition of the TOP500 list, which ranks the world's most powerful supercomputers. The newly released list illustrates that AMD powers 40% of the list, up 14% compared to 2025. AMD-powered systems now tally up to 202 on the list, up from 177 last year. New to the list is Lux, a “SI + HPC supercomputer” developed by AMD and the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), which debuted at position 14.

AMD’s representation in the top 10 is familiar. After debuting in June, China’s LineShine remains in the top slot. El Capitan, powered by AMD chips, is in the second slot, followed by Frontier in third, HPC7 in sixth, and HPC6 in eighth, the same as the most recent TOP500 list.

Lux was announced a little over a year ago in a $1 billion deal between the DOE and AMD to develop two supercomputers. This is the first of the two, and the first time it’s been on the TOP500. The system was built by HPE and developed by AMD and DOE as part of the U.S. government’s Genesis Mission. The machine is housed in Oak Ridge National Laboratory’s Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF), alongside machines like Frontier.

Lux relies on over 4,000 Instinct MI355X GPUs separated into over 500 nodes. For HPC workloads using FP64, the machine is capable of 317 PetaFLOPS. For AI training, the machine peaks at 20 ExaFLOPS of FP8, and for inference, 40 ExaFLOPS of FP4. With 288 GB of HBM3E per GPU, the entire system has access to 1.1 PB.

The machine uses AMD EPYC host CPUs — we weren’t able to find details on the specific generation— and AMD’s Pensando networking, with nodes built inside HPE’s ProLiant Compute XD685.

LingKun, LX2 304C 1.55GHz, LingQi, Kylin OS

HPE Cray EX255a, AMD 4th Gen EPYC 24C 1.8GHz, AMD Instinct MI300A, Slingshot-11, TOSS

HPE Cray EX235a, AMD Optimized 3rd Generation EPYC 64C 2GHz, AMD Instinct MI250X, Slingshot-11, HPE Cray OS

HPE Cray EX - Intel Exascale Compute Blade, Xeon CPU Max 9470 52C 2.4GHz, Intel Data Center GPU Max, Slingshot-11

BullSequana XH3000, GH Superchip 72C 3GHz, NVIDIA GH200 Superchip, Quad-Rail NVIDIA InfiniBand NDR200, RedHat Enterprise Linux

HPE Cray EX255a, AMD 4th Gen EPYC 24C 1.8GHz, AMD Instinct MI300A, Slingshot-11, RHEL 9

Microsoft NDv5, Xeon Platinum 8480C 48C 2GHz, NVIDIA H100, NVIDIA Infiniband NDR

HPE Cray EX235a, AMD Optimized 3rd Generation EPYC 64C 2GHz, AMD Instinct MI250X, Slingshot-11, RHEL 8.9

Supercomputer Fugaku, A64FX 48C 2.2GHz, Tofu interconnect D

HPE Cray EX254n, NVIDIA Grace 72C 3.1GHz, NVIDIA GH200 Superchip, Slingshot-11, HPE Cray OS

The machine has 3.5 million node-hours (one node running for one hour) per year, half of which are reserved for Genesis Mission research. Genesis Mission was established through an executive order in November 2025 with the stated goal of creating a “dedicated, coordinated national effort to unleash a new age of AI‑accelerated innovation and discovery that can solve the most challenging problems of this century.”

More specifically, the order lays out training scientific foundation models on Federal datasets, and creating “AI agents to test new hypotheses, automate research workflows, and accelerate scientific breakthroughs.” Lux is the first dedicated supercomputer for the Genesis Mission, but it joins existing machines that have been at least partially dedicated to research, including the Intel-powered Aurora at Argonne National Laboratory, which arrived fourth in the most recent TOP500.

Lux is built primarily for AI research, not as a successor to Frontier. Discovery is on track to debut as a next-gen flagship exascale supercomputer in 2028, also developed jointly by the DOE, AMD, and HPE.

#10188. Save serious money with RedPocket Mobile’s $5 Black Friday plans

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.

#10185. Researchers ‘recognize that AI will never replace HPC,’ says AMD executive — high-precision workloads will ‘exist for a very long time’ even as AI usage expands

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While AI continues to reshape industries across science and mathematics, traditional supercomputing has seemingly taken a back seat. However, AMD's Scott Tease told Tom's Hardware Premium that most HPC sites “recognize that AI will never replace HPC,” and suggests that building a heterogeneous compute stack is the best path forward to tackle bleeding-edge research, despite Nvidia pushing performance on lower-precision data types higher for AI workloads at the cost of native performance for the important double-precision FP64 format that’s critical for HPC workloads.

A heterogeneous compute stack brings together quantum, HPC, and AI under one roof, a fleet that AMD is helping pilot at Oak Ridge National Laboratory alongside IBM. Tease is AMD’s corporate vice president of HPC and sovereign AI, who joined the company earlier this year after more than a decade at Lenovo (and, before that, another 13 years at IBM). We spoke with Tease ahead of SC26 to get a read on the state of HPC, how it intersects with AI, and what’s actually driving the research community in a time when computer chips are driving headlines about international espionage plots.

FP64 is important for HPC workloads because of its precision. FP64 is a hot-button issue, largely due to how Nvidia’s GPUs have progressed over the past two generations. Or rather, how they haven't progressed. Nvidia’s latest Rubin GPU has 33 TFLOPs of FP64 compute, which is lower than the four-year-old H100’s 34 TFLOPs of FP64. For context, AMD’s year-old Instinct MI355X peaks at 78.6 TFLOPs of FP64 compute, while its upcoming HPC-angled MI430X peaks at 288 TFLOPs.

Nvidia has leaned on emulation to drive FP64 performance higher, a tactic that AMD is now pushing back on, but regardless, it’s easy to see why the HPC world could feel left behind in this new AI era. AI is defined by fuzzy, low-precision data formats, which is diametrically opposed to the accuracy of high-precision FP64. And, over the last generations of hardware from the big green giant that is Nvidia, FP64 performance has seemingly stalled in favor of driving low-precision data formats for AI workloads.

“Maybe a few years ago, people in HPC might have felt a little threatened by AI because it was taking some of the limelight,” Tease told Tom’s Hardware Premium. “But I think now they’ve come to realize that AI is an essential part of scientific discovery today. It’s just, how are you going to deploy it?”

That central question recontextualizes the apparent conflict between AI compute and HPC compute. Tease pointed to researchers spawning dozens of agents to explore viable paths for a given intent, while still leaving the complex simulations for a traditional FP64-driven HPC fleet. “...to try to do that with old approaches where you have islands of CPUs or islands of GPUs or something like that, and they're very different. They're not interconnected. It's just not going to be able to keep up,” Tease said.

One of the challenges right now is actually connecting those systems, which is an area AMD is exploring with its recent collaboration with IBM and Oak Ridge National Laboratory to deploy AMD’s “classical” computing stack in the Umbra HPC system alongside IBM’s 129-qubit Quantum Nighthawk quantum computer. The systems will connect to the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility, allowing researchers to “explore how integrated quantum, AI, and scientific computing can accelerate scientific discoveries and national security.”

If the HPC crowd felt slighted by AI, you can imagine how quantum researchers probably feel.

Regardless, Tease says the future of scientific computing looks like a unified fleet that brings together AI, HPC, and quantum. “There [are] going to be a lot of problems where quantum will not be the be-all, end-all. There are some problems like human health, chemistry, where quantum’s going to allow us to do things we really can’t do with classical computing today. So having those things coexist, having them in a single workload, is where we want to get to.”

Scheduling workloads for HPC is a solved game, with open-source schedulers like Slurm driving much of the TOP500 to properly schedule workloads on supercomputers. Tease described something that goes deeper to connect these systems. “What we want to be able to do is not only do those kinds of ‘where should it go, here or there’ for HPC, we want to figure out when AI could be applied to it, when [GPU acceleration is] appropriate… and also when is it best to go in a quantum environment?”

The Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility is a test bed for figuring out how to hook these systems together properly. The press release explicitly states that it’s a “shared environment to evaluate the architectures, software, and workflows that future hybrid quantum systems will require.” The system is a co-design initiative with IBM, bringing together quantum and classical computing into one system.

With the system, researchers are exploring how to accelerate quantum error correction with classical systems, a critical step in quantum computing to weed out environmental influences. The goal is ultimately to accelerate quantum systems in the Quantum Genesis Q competition, an initiative launched by the U.S. Department of Energy that will provide up to $215 million in funding for quantum research.

By late 2028, the competition calls for quantum computers “with at least 100 logical qubits that are capable of performing hundreds of millions [of] fault-tolerant operations.” IBM describes the error correction research that will be performed at Oak Ridge as a “key component” in driving toward that goal. Separately, AMD announced last week an investment in quantum outfit PsiQuantum to provide CPUs, GPUs, and FPGAs to drive the company’s first fault-tolerant quantum system.

#10183. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

The first two tests in Discord: a question, then a file written with a tool. The user name is blurred.

The mistakes showed up when the model had to check something:

After the restart, the bot claimed it had lost the earlier session. When asked to use its read tool, it read the file correctly.

The fix for the first problem was simple. When I wrote "use your read tool to open homelab-test.md", the model opened the file and listed all three points correctly. Naming the tool made it check instead of guess.

The model listed four files and missed the test file and a folder, because it answered from its prompt.

Based on this test, a local 9B model on one consumer GPU works for:

If you need better answers, OpenClaw also works with hosted models, but then your prompts leave your machine.

In this test, qwen3.5:9b used 6.3 GB and the qwen3-embedding:4b memory model used 5.1 GB. Together the two models needed 11.4 GB.

The model had to load into GPU memory first, which took 59 seconds of the 70-second reply. Ollama keeps a model loaded for 5 minutes by default, and the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE setting changes that.

Every request includes OpenClaw's system prompt, its tool definitions and its workspace files. A one-line question became an 18,415-token prompt in this test.

Yes. A heartbeat automation runs every 30 minutes and calls the model even when nobody sends a message.

It handled questions, arithmetic and file creation correctly in this test. It sometimes answered from its prompt instead of using its tools. When it wrongly said its memory was lost, asking it to use its read tool made it check the file and answer correctly.

#10182. Someone rebuilt old Android versions in your browser, and it’s an instant nostalgia hit [Gallery]

The “Android Time Machine” is a fun project that puts Android versions into your browser, giving you a quick and easy chance to play around with older versions for a quick hit of nostalgia.

Built on the back of AOSP builds for classic Nexus devices, “Android Time Machine” lets you quickly access older Android versions. Specifically, Android 2.1 Eclair through Android 7.1.2 Nougat.

The simulator runs within the shell of a matching Nexus smartphone on a desktop browser, or to fit your actual smartphone if you run it on Android.

Versions and simulated smartphones include:

The best part here is how it works on your actual smartphone. When launched in fullscreen, it scales to match your phone’s screen – meaning it fits even devices like the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s smaller outer display (it just shows the desktop version when open).

Since this is running through the browser and not an actual Android build, you won’t get a perfect replication. Performance is a little hit or miss, especially with certain animations, but it works really well overall.

The browser even simulates results from each era (oh, to go back to when Google Search wasn’t an absolute mess). Similarly, Google apps and the camera are filled with placeholders since the web app can’t access the actual camera or connect to the internet to sign into a Google account.

Seeing things like the boot animations and live wallpapers from classic Nexus phones, as well as peeking back at lost apps like Google+ and Play Music, is just such a fun little piece of nostalgia.

“Android Time Machine” is free, and the source code is available on GitHub.

The Bureau of Industry and Security has released an interim final rule to ensure physical restrictions cannot be bypassed by using cloud-based infrastructure.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

New steps have been taken to ensure that countries and companies that are not on whitelists that would allow them to import advanced AI hardware cannot use cloud-based workarounds. The U.S. Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) has today unveiled an interim final rule, or IFR, in collaboration with the Department of Treasury and the National Security Council, aimed at backing up existing limitations on the export of hardware built by companies like Nvidia to non-whitelisted companies based in countries such as China. "The rule is designed to ensure that existing restrictions on access to sensitive American technology cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements" using data centers in third-party countries, a statement explains.

Nvidia, for example, is only allowed to export its high-performance AI chips to companies that are on a whitelist. But amid ongoing claims of black market GPU sales and a recent crackdown on overseas AI GPU rentals from China, it has become clear that these restrictions perhaps aren't as robust as they were intended to be. Nvidia Blackwell Ultra B300 GPUs were even recently discovered in a data center used by China Telecom.

But while blocking the sale of physical goods is one thing, the cloud-based workaround is another entirely. Nvidia itself told data center operators earlier this month that they must limit remote access to their services — 51% of their business must come from non-Chinese companies. But, again, it's unclear how effective that approach has been in ensuring access to powerful AI capabilities doesn't fall into the wrong hands, even if they're accessed remotely rather than in person.

The new BIS rule means that overseas data centers will be required to do more to limit access to their services where U.S-based technology is being used. Restrictions will also be placed on data centers that supply remote access to companies whose headquarters are deemed to be in higher-risk countries, such as China, Russia, and Iran. A license will also be required for anyone trying to export advanced chips to companies with headquarters in Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, or Brazil. The new requirements will officially go into effect in 90 days.

This latest rule comes against a backdrop of ongoing uncertainty over America's AI crown jewel, Nvidia. Jensen Huang, the company's CEO, resigned after confirming an affair, but is now potentially in line for a quick-fire comeback, despite the fact that the woman involved in the affair is an alleged Chinese spy.

#10177. Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

A live walkthrough of the Agents CLI eval loop, from a first run to regression tracking, model-backend experiments, and automated alerting

Building an agent that works on a demo is a different problem from building an agent that stays working across model upgrades, tool changes, and new conversation shapes. The first takes a weekend; the second takes a loop. A team writes test cases, runs them, grades them, reads what failed, changes the agent, and runs the cases again. If the score goes up, the change was good. If it goes down, revert. Over weeks of this, the agent inches toward the ceiling of what the model, tools, and prompt can do together. The quality-engineering name for this is hill-climbing.

The Agents CLI eval namespace was built around this loop. The commands are small and framework-agnostic: eval generate runs the agent against a dataset of cases, eval grade scores the generated traces, eval compare diffs two run results side by side, eval analyze clusters failures into themes, and eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt.

Below: baseline → regression → model swap → scheduling and alerting.

The scaffold used throughout is the LangChain template that ships with Agents CLI v1.8.0. The setup is a single command:

The scaffold ships with an eval dataset at tests/eval/datasets/basic-dataset.json, a config at tests/eval/eval_config.yaml, and a custom LLM-as-judge metric at tests/eval/response_quality.py. The config declares the metric to run:

The metric grades each response on a 1-to-5 scale for accuracy, relevance, and clarity, using gemini-3.8-flash as the judge. The default dataset has a single trivial case, which is not enough for a hill-climb. The first thing we do is expand it to four cases that probe different behaviors:

Four cases: a greeting, a weather question the mock tool handles with the hardcoded foggy response, a weather question for a city the tool defaults on, and an off-topic question that the agent has to decide how to handle. This is a small dataset by design; the hill-climbing loop pays off when the dataset is small enough to run on every change.

With the dataset in place, we run eval generate to drive the agent against each case, then eval grade to score the resulting traces:

Mean 4.0 out of 5 across four cases, with a standard deviation of 1.41. That stdev tells us immediately that the cases are not scoring uniformly, which is a healthier starting point than a flat 5.0 across the board. A flat 5.0 either means the agent is perfect (unlikely) or the dataset is too easy to show variation. 4.0 with real spread is a hill to climb.

To demonstrate the loop, we introduce a realistic regression. A developer working across several agent projects accidentally pastes the wrong system prompt into our weather agent: a chef persona meant for a different codebase. In app/agent.py:

The change compiles, the agent still runs, and nothing in the pre-eval pipeline (lint, unit tests, type checks) catches it. Re-running the same eval generate plus eval grade sequence produces:

Mean 2.75, down from 4.0. The judge agrees with our intuition that a chef persona on a weather agent is a worse product than a general-purpose helpful assistant. The stdev also tightened from 1.41 to 0.96, consistent with the chef prompt pushing most cases toward the same mediocre range.

A 1.25-point drop on a 1-to-5 scale is substantial, and the kind of regression that can slip through code review when the diff looks small ("one string literal changed"). The loop surfaced the change at all, with a specific number attached to it, before the agent reached production.

Mean scores are a start; the per-case diff is where the hill-climb happens. eval compare reads two result files and produces a structured JSON diff:

The output lists which keys changed and which stayed the same:

The differences object has the full per-case breakdown with the baseline and candidate values side by side. For a hill-climbing workflow, we typically pipe the output through jq or a small Python script to extract the signal; the raw JSON is the complete evidence.

Walking the two result files and lining up the scores per case shows where the regression landed:

Three cases dropped, one held. The most important of the three is sf_weather: the chef persona pulled the model's attention away from the registered get_weather tool, so the agent stopped calling it and started making up weather values. Unit tests can't catch this: the code runs, the tool is still registered, but the model's behavior around it has shifted. Only an end-to-end eval that scores the response in context surfaces this.

The mean drop by itself (−1.25) is a clear signal, but the per-case breakdown is what tells the developer where to look. A one-line system-prompt change propagated through three different cases in three different ways: greeting lost a point on tone, sf_weather lost two on tool usage, off_topic lost two on scope creep.

A common hill-climbing question is whether a cheaper or faster model would do the job acceptably. For the LangChain template, the model is defined in app/agent.py:

We swap gemini-3.8-flash for gemini-2.5-flash (restoring the helpful system prompt first), re-run eval generate plus eval grade, and compare the two result files. The mean drops from 4.00 to 2.75, the same magnitude as the chef-prompt regression, but the per-case breakdown tells a completely different story:

The cheaper model matched 3.8-flash exactly on the greeting and rome_weather cases and lost a point on sf_weather (slightly less polished phrasing). The whole visible drop traces to off_topic, where the cheaper model refused with a hedge about the event "not having happened yet" (a training-cutoff artifact), and the judge marked it harshly.

The lesson is that the summary number is misleading. Look only at the mean and you'd call gemini-2.5-flash as bad as the chef prompt. Look at the per-case breakdown and you'd call it a reasonable swap for most of the agent's workload, with one case (handling out-of-scope questions about recent events) exposing a real limitation. If the production distribution of user questions matches the first three cases more than the fourth, the swap is worth considering. If off_topic-style questions are common, it is not.

Two caveats on this experiment. First, model swaps interact with the prompt: a prompt tuned for a stronger model often leaks capability when ported to a weaker one, so the right comparison is "weaker model with a prompt tuned for it" versus "stronger model with its own tuned prompt." Second, the Vertex AI Eval Service calls a judge model to grade the traces, and the judge is held constant across the comparison. Changing the judge is a different experiment and should be done separately.

Agents CLI does not ship a scheduler, but the eval commands are designed to drop into standard infrastructure. The pattern we can use here is a cron job or Cloud Scheduler trigger that runs eval generate plus eval grade on a cadence and writes the results to a persistent location:

A nightly run against a stable regression dataset catches drift from model updates, data changes in the agent's tools, and dependencies that shift under the agent's feet. For a production agent, the scheduled run also captures the model vendor's quiet upgrades, which can move the agent's behavior without any change on our side. The dataset for the scheduled run is typically larger than the one used in the inner loop (dozens to hundreds of cases rather than a few), since it only runs once a day.

The scheduled run produces a result file every day, but a file nobody looks at is not an alert. The pattern here can be a wrapper script that compares each new run's mean against a baseline (the previous run, or a committed known-good) and exits non-zero when the drop exceeds a threshold. The non-zero exit is what triggers the alert from the surrounding scheduler.

Running it against our regression demo (baseline 4.0, candidate 2.75 from the chef-prompt run):

And against an identical pair (self-compare):

The non-zero exit code is what a cron job, Cloud Scheduler, or GitHub Actions step picks up to send the alert.

For ADK projects, eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt using the GEPA framework (not currently supported for the LangChain extension). The command takes a dataset and a target metric, proposes prompt variants, grades each one, keeps the variants that improve the metric, and iterates. The output is a set of candidate prompts ranked by their eval score, which a human then reviews and promotes to the agent definition.

Two things to understand about eval optimize. First, it is scoped to prompt optimization, not to arbitrary agent changes: the agent's tools, structure, and model stay fixed. Second, GEPA sometimes finds genuine improvements and sometimes finds prompt variants that score higher on the specific metric by overfitting to its wording. The reviewer is the control. For projects where the loop applies, the discipline is to run eval optimize on the regression dataset (where improvements are measurable) and to review the candidate prompts carefully before promotion.

A few patterns and anti-patterns from running this loop ourselves and watching teams do the same.

Lead with a small, representative dataset, not a comprehensive one. The temptation when starting an eval is to try to cover every edge case the agent will ever see. The result is a dataset that takes an hour to run and that nobody runs on a change. Four cases that run in under a minute, as above, exercise the main behaviors and get run on every change. A regression dataset of three hundred cases that runs for an hour becomes a release gate at best and shelfware at worst.

Snapshot result files, not scores. The score is a summary; the result file is the evidence. When a regression surfaces three weeks after a prompt change, having the full result JSON from before and after the change (not just the mean score) is what lets eval compare show the specific case that moved. The per-case breakdown in Step 4 above would not have been possible from scores alone.

Separate the dataset from its expectations. The dataset is the inputs. The eval config is the expectations. They evolve on different cadences: the dataset grows as new real-world cases surface in production, and the config evolves as the team's definition of "good" matures. Keeping them in separate files, both committed to version control, makes it clear which half changed when a score moves.

Treat summary metrics with suspicion. The model-swap experiment in Step 5 is the clearest example. The mean dropped by half a point, but the per-case breakdown showed the drop was concentrated in one case while the others held or improved. A team that only looked at the mean would have made the wrong call.

More evals do not automatically lead to more fixes. A recent controlled study we ran found that coding agents using agents-cli run four times as many evals as coding agents without it, but change the agent after an eval about as often as the baseline. The behavior gain comes from what the skills put into the build at the start, not from the inner iteration. Evals are necessary for quality, but the quality comes from acting on what the evals show.

Scaffold an Agents CLI project and run the first eval loop in a handful of commands:

The full eval namespace is documented at google.github.io/agents-cli/cli. The source code, including the first-party eval primitives, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

#10176. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10174. China retains fastest supercomputer in the world crown as AI data centers soak up new hardware

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

As hyperscalers want more AI, traditional supercomputers take a back seat.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China's LineShine remains the world's highest-performing supercomputer, according to the 68th edition of the Top500 list published at SC26 in Chicago on Monday. For the first time in decades, the supercomputer ranking saw exceptionally few additions despite unprecedented demand for high-performance computing hardware, as the vast amounts of compute resources deployed these days are destined for artificial intelligence (AI) data centers rather than traditional supercomputers.

China's LineShine, installed at the National Supercomputer Center in Shenzhen, offers sustained FP64 compute performance of 2.198 FP64 ExaFLOPS in the High Performance Linpack (HPL) benchmark at 42.22 MW. The machine relies on the homegrown LingKun platform with 304-core Armx9-based LX2 processors clocked at 1.55 GHz (13,789,440 cores in total) and the proprietary LingQi interconnect. Unlike the other four exascale-class supercomputers, LineShine does not use any compute accelerators and exclusively relies on CPUs, which is largely why it only gains 3.6X performance when moving from FP64 to mixed-precision data, which is lower than systems that use low-precision accelerators.

America's most powerful El Capitan supercomputer at the Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory relies on AMD's Instinct MI300A accelerated processing units (APUs), and the HPE Cray EX255a platform delivers 1.809 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 29.69 MW, which makes it significantly more power-efficient than its rival from China. The Frontier supercomputer at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory, based on AMD's 3rd Generation EPYC processors and Instinct MI250X compute GPUs, achieves 1.353 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 24.61 MW. Meanwhile, Aurora at the Argonne National Laboratory, which uses Intel's Xeon CPU Max 9470 processors and Data Center GPU Max accelerators, offers 1.012 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 38.70 MW.

All three American exascale supercomputers use HPE’s Cray EX platforms and Slingshot-11 interconnect.

Germany’s Jupiter Booster at the Jülich Supercomputing Center, which employs Nvidia’s GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips, the BullSequana XH3000 platform, and Nvidia’s quad-rail InfiniBand NDR200 interconnect, delivers 1.0 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 15.79 MW and remains the most energy-efficient exascale supercomputer on the list.

Remarkably, neither the 10 highest-performing supercomputers nor the 10 most energy-efficient machines have changed their positions since June.

Only 27 new supercomputers (5.4% of all machines on the list) made it to the November edition of the Top 500, the lowest number since the project was founded in 1993. The most powerful newcomer is South Korea's Hanggang at the KISTI Supercomputing Center, which occupies the 12th position with 342.6 FP64 PFLOPS. The machine uses Nvidia's GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips and HPE's Cray EX254n platform. Hanggang also ranks eighth in the HPCG benchmark with 4.29 HPCG PFLOPS.

The aggregate HPL performance of the world's 500 highest-performing supercomputers increased by a modest 4.4% over the past six months to 19.57 FP64 ExaFLOPS. Nonetheless, it now takes a machine with 2.857 PFLOPS of sustained FP64 performance to qualify for the TOP500, up 7.3% from 2.662 PFLOPS in June. The performance of the systems ranked 50th and 100th rose from 49.85 PFLOPS to 55.81 PFLOPS (12%) and from 21.85 PFLOPS to 22.74 PFLOPS, respectively. Also, there are now 27 machines that deliver at least 100 PFLOPS of FP64 performance, up from 25 in June, whereas the number of supercomputers equipped with compute accelerators increased from 275 to 291 (58.2%).

The historically low number of new entrants to the Top 500 is particularly noteworthy given the unprecedented scale of investments in advanced computing infrastructure these days. According to estimates by Epoch AI cited by the Top 500 authors, the amount of AI compute capacity added globally every quarter has increased by 2.5X per year. Essentially, the industry's priorities have shifted from construction of machines capable of solving exceptionally complex scientific problems to deployment of vast amounts of compute capacity for commercial AI workloads.

In fact, the world's installed AI compute capacity already exceeds the equivalent of 15 million Nvidia H100 GPUs, according to Epoch AI. Furthermore, as of the fourth quarter of 2025, five major American hyperscalers accounted for an estimated 71% of the world's AI compute resources, much of which was deployed in massive clusters. While demand for advanced computing hardware is at an all-time high, it appears that the vast majority of new hardware never makes it to the world's most known supercomputer ranking.

Technology has changed rapidly over the last few decades. While we've got hardware that's so much more powerful than what came 10 or 20 years ago, there's still a lot of enjoyment to be had in the classics. That's why there's a huge market for retro hardware, from game controllers designed to look like classic gamepads from old consoles, to full re-releases of old machines, with plenty of fun (and well-priced) accessories in between.

If you're buying for a retro aficionado, you're going to want to pick up something that's fun to use and has a serious nostalgic edge. There's a whole host of gear out there for those who prefer an '80s or '90s aesthetic, rather than the products we live with today.

● Check out all these retro PC gift ideas on Amazon

You can relive the glory days of yesteryear today with these retro gift ideas. We've put together some of the best ideas for a wistful techie who can upgrade their setup, with old-school designs ready for today's hardware. Check out the options we've hand-picked for you below.

This miniature recreation of a classic NeoGeo arcade cabinet includes 40 games (the average price of a Neo Geo game in the 1990s was $200, $8000 for all 40 games!) and includes classics like Metal Slug, The King of Fighters and of course, Fatal Fury. The console has scaled versions of the original controls, a 3.5-inch screen, and runs from a USB-C power supply.

Need a bigger screen? You can connect via HDMI. External controllers and NeoGeo Mini Game controllers (available separately) can be used to retro game from the sofa!

This Game Boy-style console is tiny (0.7 x 0.3 x 1.2 inches!) and is powered by the Raspberry Pi Pico's RP2040 microcontroller. Programmable via a web interface, you can make your own games and demos to share with others.

This gorgeous, retro-inspired N Edition Retro Cube 2 wireless speaker from 8BitDo offers stereo sound, with support for 2.4 GHz and Bluetooth connectivity, along with USB for wired use, and includes a wireless charging dock.

A 1:1 scale replica of the gaming handheld that dominated the 1990s. This is a glorious piece of retro tech, preserved in Lego form for your shelves.

Nintendo's Game Boy is an iconic piece of tech, and this Lego model is a replica of the great handheld. It comes with two cartridges, Super Mario Land and The Legend of Zelda: Link's Awakening. The screen can be replaced with a lenticular screen that recreates the original console's pixels.

This retro Hagibis display comes with a 3.5-inch IPS LCD with a native resolution of 960 x 640, although it can scale up to 2,560 x 1,600 if you like punishing your eyes. It also includes several USB-A and USB-C ports with Gen 3.1 speeds, an HDMI port, as well as SD and TF card readers.

Another Macintosh-style design, but this one is a little simpler. This isn't an add-on: it's a classic retro desk clock that takes you back to 1984. The LCD display works as an alarm clock, nightlight, temperature sensor, and more. It's also got a 'working' floppy disk attachment that, while decorative, lets you simulate a Macintosh-style bootup, just for funsies.

Want to go back to the '90s with a 95-style PC ornament? #10168. Samsung’s ‘Galaxy Studio’ headphones leak with touch controls, two colors

We’re only a couple of months away from our next Galaxy Unpacked, and while the expanded Galaxy S27 series is practically guaranteed to show up, the real fun tends to lie in whatever other gadgets show up alongside Samsung’s next flagship smartphone release. Based on some new leaks, it’s sure looking like the next mobile accessory on the company’s docket is a return to over-ear wireless headphones, and so far, they’re sounding pretty promising.

Samsung hasn’t produced over-ear headphones in about a decade — under its own name, at least — but it looks like that’s about to change. We hadn’t heard much in the way of details on the company’s next audio release, rumored to be named ‘Galaxy Studio,’ but a fresh leak courtesy of SamMobile is finally changing that. We’ll have to keep waiting for full renders, but these leaked graphics are giving us a general idea of what they might look like, plus a couple of key features.

Unsurprisingly, Galaxy Studio will include ANC, complete with a dedicated button to toggle the setting between its usual modes. Volume controls also could come in the shape of physical buttons, but playback controls appear to be relegated to a touch-sensitive area on its earpads, something that’s become relatively standard among the flagship headphone competition. SamMobile also reports it’ll come in “at least” black and red colorways, while a leak from earlier this month suggested rotational earcups.

Again, I don’t want to read too much into these leaked graphics, as it’s always possible the final release has a handful of changes. Based on these images, though, I’m pleasantly surprised to see Samsung avoiding an attempt at copying Apple’s AirPods Max design. The larger cups, combined with their ability to twist and turn for easy storage, sounds much closer to most (but not all) of Sony’s over-ear efforts across the last decade or so, and when it comes to headphones, there are much, much worse choices you could look to for inspiration. If Samsung can hit the right price on these — or bundle them with one of their ever-more-expensive smartphones — they could be a real winner.

#10165. Your SSL certificates can now last 200 days at most: The real risk isn’t renewal

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every SSL certificate comes with a limited lifespan. Following the latest update from the CA/Browser Forum, the maximum lifetime of a certificate has fallen from 398 days to 200, and it will drop to 100 days in March 2027 and 47 days in 2029. Renewal is no longer an annual chore. It’s an ongoing task that only stays manageable with automation.

So what does this mean if you already look after sites with SSL certificates, and will it bring drastic changes? For many setups, very little will change. For others, one missed renewal can mean an outage. This article explains what’s changing and what to do if you’re in the second group.

Today, an SSL certificate is valid for 200 days at most. The limit applies when a certificate is issued, so the ones you already hold keep their original expiration date.

Where you feel it depends on how your certificates renew. Sites on managed hosting platforms or behind a content delivery network (CDN) usually renew automatically already, often every 90 days, so the new limit changes little. The pressure lands on setups where someone buys a certificate once a year and installs it by hand. At 47 days, that becomes about 12 renewals a year.

A certificate is a promise from a certificate authority that a key really belongs to a domain, and that promise gets less reliable the longer it runs. If a private key leaks, or a certificate is issued by mistake, it stays usable until it expires. Cutting the lifetime shrinks that window, so a problem that once lingered for over a year is now fixed in weeks.

Shorter lifetimes also make revocation, the process for canceling a bad certificate, far less critical. Revocation has never worked perfectly, but a certificate that expires within weeks matters much less if a cancellation doesn’t reach every browser.

Another reason is about people. A task done once a year is easy to forget and hard to check from outside. Browser makers have pushed for years to move the industry onto automatic renewal, on the logic that a process that runs every few weeks gets tested constantly. If it breaks, you notice quickly instead of discovering it on the day the certificate expires.

For most people, the first effect is simply more work. A certificate that once needed attention every year will soon need it every few weeks. Plus, each renewal is a small chance for something to go wrong. Multiply that by dozens of sites, mail servers, and devices, and the chance of one slipping through grows quickly.

The safety nets are also thinner. If you relied on the renewal emails, nothing is watching your certificates now. Additionally, with a shorter life, there’s less time between this should have renewed and this has expired.

An expired certificate doesn’t make a site slow or dated. It makes it unreachable for most visitors. Chrome, Firefox, and Safari all show a full-page warning that the connection isn’t private, and most people leave instead of clicking through. If the site uses HSTS, a setting that tells browsers to connect only over secure links, there is often no way to click through at all, and visitors are blocked outright until the certificate is fixed.

The damage doesn’t stop at the home page. Checkouts, contact forms, and logins that depend on the secure connection fail along with it, often without an error message that points at the real cause. Other systems break, too, because apps and integrations that call your site over a secure link refuse to connect to an expired certificate.

Nothing about your site has to change for any of this to happen. A date passes, and a site that worked on Friday is down on Monday. In a 47-day lifetime, that date comes around about every six weeks.

Automatic renewal tools do the heavy lifting well. The failures happen around them:

Look at the five failures above and you’ll see what they share. Each one happens quietly. The renewal job ran, or looked like it did, and nobody checked what the server was actually serving afterwards. Automation can tell you it tried. It can’t tell you the result reached your visitors.

What closes that gap is a check that never relies on your server’s own account of events. Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring does exactly that. It reads the certificate installed on your web server from the cloud, the way a visitor’s browser would, and alerts you before it expires, using thresholds you choose. If a renewal fails quietly, you hear about it from the monitor and not from a customer.

For each certificate, it records the issuing authority, the owner, the issue date, the expiration date, and the days remaining, including on servers that host several certificates behind one address. That last number matters more as lifespans shrink. When a certificate lasts 47 days, the days left is the figure that tells you a renewal is overdue, and you can set your warning against it using the quarter-of-the-lifetime rule covered next.

Once a monitor is watching your certificates, the next question is when it should speak up.

Most expiration alerts are set to a fixed number of days: warn at 30 days left and escalate at seven. That works for a certificate that lasts a year and renews with a month to spare. It breaks when the whole certificate lasts for a limited number of days.

Here’s why: a healthy certificate renews with about a third of its life still left. If your alert appears earlier than that, it goes off on certificates that are doing nothing wrong, and soon everyone ignores it. As lifespans shrink, a fixed 30-day alert will start doing exactly that.

The fix is to set the alerts based on lateness, not on how close the expiry date is. Warn when a quarter of the lifetime is left, since a renewal is overdue by then, and escalate when a tenth is left. Because these are shares of the lifetime, they keep working, however short lifespans get.

The next cut lands on March 15, 2027, so a few checks now will save you from scrambling later:

Shorter lifespans won’t break your site, but a missed renewal will, so fix the quiet failures now while a mistake still costs an afternoon to fix, not a Monday morning outage affecting lots of customers.

If you’d like a second pair of eyes on your certificates, you can start a 30-day free trial of Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring.

#10162. Gemini rolling out 66 new regional voices on Android and iOS, including 10+ UK dialects

Following a preview at Google I/O, Google is rolling out 66 new regional voices to the Gemini app on both Android and iOS today. The expansion introduces tailored voice options across U.S. English, U.K. English, Indian English, Hindi, Brazilian Portuguese, Indonesian, and Korean.

For users in the UK, the update brings over 10 distinct English voice options covering a wide range of regional cadences. The lineup includes Geordie, Scouse, Brummie, Scottish, Welsh, Northern Irish, Modern RP, and Multicultural London English (MLE).

This might help reduce the issues that sometimes still occur when English accents, in particular, morph into a hybrid of Australian or even American that has been going on for a while with Gemini Live. We have not yet been able to test to confirm this has been resolved with the update. As someone with a regional English accent, this extra representation with Gemini voices is quite welcome.

According to Google, these voices are tuned for natural pronunciation, pitch, and everyday local rhythm rather than exaggerated stereotypes. Because the focus is on cadence over slang, Gemini will sound like a local without filling its text responses with forced street slang. Once selected, your chosen voice carries across the entire app, applying to both Gemini Live conversations and standard text-to-speech playback when tapping the “Listen” icon on chat responses.

You can preview and select the new options today by tapping the three-dot menu inside Gemini Live, or by heading into the Gemini app settings and selecting “Gemini’s voice.” It’s worth noting that the voices aren’t explicitly labeled by city or region in the app menu, and Gemini isn’t programmed to self-identify which accent it is using if you ask it directly.

Instead, Google notes that locally rooted prompts about food, culture, or sport, such as asking for the best pub in Sheffield for a Sunday roast – my suggestion is not a pub; it’s Turnip & Thyme on Eccleshall Road, FWIW. Or another suggestion from big G is where to find proper ingredients for a traditional Scouse stew; the company says these will naturally bring out the warmth and rhythm of the new dialects.

I’d suggest trying to ask where to get the best pattie and chips in Hull or where to get the best pint in Leeds (it’s Whitelocks).
